WOMEN'S GHAT.

-Holideys.—WO shall soon be discussing the problem of the holidays ; when and where to go, and the best method of spending the annual vacation. People's tastes and circumstances vary so much, that there is no general rule applicable to holidays. One is for a sea voyage, another prefers climbing mountains, a third wants to sit all day on the sands, a fourth must have a motor tour, and a fifth thinks nothing so beneficial as a prolonged sojourn in bed. We are air apt to forget that our own ideas are not necessarily everybody else's, and unfortunately, not many people are found to agree with the medical journal whieli has been advocating the spring-time as the best season of the year for a holiday. Yet there is a good deal to be said in its favour. The country is at its brightest and freshest, the weather is often more enjoyable than in the sultriest months of the Tear, and there is not the overcrowding that is ,Experienced at the holiday resorts in August. Every- one would benefit if the holidays were spread over more months than they are, making more use of May and June in this respect, when native is at her best. Games as Character Builders.—That games and bobbies are character-builders is a theory now generally accepted by educationists. According to the new school of thought, archery, tennis, and hockey, are games to teach agility and quickness of mind at well as of body, besides also of training the eye. The school- girl who is naturally slow and deliberate will be mere inclined to play croquet, but at high schools, where sports and games are a regular part of the curiculum, she would be encouraged to join in the games which would make her active and quick to seize opportunities. Similarly, some doctors are advising women to go in for motor-driving, to teach self-reliance, alertness, mental agility, nerve, and readiness of decision. There is no doubt that practice of this kind does help in the formation of character,end nowadays girls have almost as many opportunities far outdoor games as boys. In all the parks and public recreation grounds under con- trol of the London County Council, girls are allowed the use of the cricket practice and match pitches on the same conditions as boys, and one can only imagine the astonishment such a regulation would have created fifty or even twenty-five years ago. Concerning Wedding Presents.—We are often reminded how surprised our ancestresses would to if they could see cr hear this or that, and Si ithout doubt they would be very mach surprised if they cotrldwi.ness the display of gifts to the present-day bride. Upon the occasion of a recent Society wedding, the bride received revere' " cebinets of cigarettes," cigarette cases, gold and silver, all inset with gems, cigarette holders and match boxes, and other dainty paraphernalia of a cigarette smoker. Then, the motor eas taken the place of the sober brougham, and the gramophone that of the piano, unless the recipient is really musical. Again, a state of affairs that would r.o doubt greatly puzzle and possibly anger our ancestresses, would he the invariable re-setting of the family jewels. In olden times, as her mother-in-law had worn her jewels, so the young matron was expected to wear them, and the process of altering their settine, and briogieg them up-to-date, was unheard of. With regard to the fluplicetion of wedding gifts, there is less chance of this than. formerly, becauie so many people now com- bine to give a mot or car, a rope of pearls, or gifts of lesser magnitude. When, however, a bride does find herself with more duplicates than she needs or desires, elle either quietly changes them for something else, or lays them aside for presentation to prospective brides and bridegrooms later on. Reading in Red.—ln Germany, it has been found that music is beneficial to hospital patients, providing it is of an appropriate kind. So also we are told. literature -may he good or bad for invalids, according to its character. Influenza eatients are advised to read "Pickwick Papers," or the works of Mark Twain, and various kinds of mentel food are susgested for different. ailments, but the "thrilling shocker," and books which excite the emotions should be generally avoided. It would be cruel to deprive the invalid of reading matter but otherwise the practice of reading' in bed is not to be commended. It is liable to induce myopia in the case of young persons, owing to the necessity for hold- ing the book too close to the eyes, and if one is in good health, reading in bed invariably means stealing from some of the hours which onglit to be devoted to sleep, of the active work of the day. Insomnia ltemedies.—Dean- Hole related a story of a former Lord Lauderdale. who suffered badly from insomnia and to whom nothing but the soothing voice of the Rector brought sleep. Another narrative relates to Bishop Creighton, who is said to have told Lord Rosebery that if he wanted sleep, all he had to do was to start reading a sermon, which brought sleep in a few minutes. According to the story, probably apocryphal, Lord Rosebery replied that it was his prac. ice to choose the lesser of two evils, end he preferred want of sleep to reading sermons. Similarly, when e are told that 'this or that is the best method of inducing sleep, we are reminded that the treatment ehich helps one person who is suffering from insomnia, is generally altogether useless to another. A Motor Tour through Eneland.—" Set in Silver" is the title of the now novel by Mr. end Mrs. C. N. -Williamson, which is to be published by Messrs. Methuen on May oth. 'I he book describes a motor tour through England—end human nature and photogriphy are blended with all the old thrill and humour of these well-known authors. The title is from John of Gannt's dying speech, in which be speaks of England as a precious gem set in a silver seal. Dress Problems for the Short and Stout—The fashion journals make all women young and slim, end elegant, and pay little regard to the ,problems of dress for the , short, stout, and—dare we whisper it—the middle-aged. 1 To the latter, the clinging, hipless,high-sveisted modes, have presented many difficulties, but with the return of the natural waist, some of them at least, have dis- appeared. Moreover, modisf es are aeeain insisting upon raising the skirt band behind, and pulling it well dewn in front, a notion becoming to all, but of especial value -to the too abundant woman. New Backles.—With the return of the natural waist, belts have come to the fore aastin, and great attention is being paid to buckles. First and foremost come the buckles of gold, engraved with the wearer's monognem and so shaped to the waist that they tit snugly. Next in favour perhaps come the buckles of enamel on gold or silver, and those in nouveau art design set with semi- precious stones. Ceral is much seen just now in con- nection with buckles of dull silver, or steel, and another charming combination is effected by dull silver and topaz. To Brighten a Carpet.—To bring up the colour of a carpet—after well beating and brushing—a capital plan is to add a little oxgall (obteinable from all butchers who kill on the premises) to a pail of water, wring a clean flannel very tightly out of it, and rub well into the pile. Plenty of air should be permitted to circulate in the room during the process of rubbing and for some time afterwards, moreover the carpet should not be trodden upon until perfectly dry.

EFFECT OF FOOD TAXATION.

Tariff Reformers do not seem to have stopped to think out the true effect of even the smallest possible taxation of the staple articles of everyday food. Much less have they contemplated the truth that wherever small food taxes have been imposed in Protectionist countries they have Jilways, in course of time, developed into larger food taxes. , FOOD TAXATION .FALLS ON THE POOR.

Let us assume, however, that the food taxes are at' first limited to 5 per cent., or 13. on each sovereign's worth. How will the 5 per cent. duties affect the hundreds of thou-sands of families in this country who have to live on a total income of £1 per week or less? It has been clearly proved that such a family must spend on the average at least two-thirds of its total income on the simplest and most economical/kinds of food.

_ A tax of 5 per cent. on the food would be Bd. per week. "A mere trifle," says the enthusiastic Tariff Reformer. But we have to remember that house-rent must come out of the .g.l per week before any food is bought. The smallest rent that can fairly be allowed, even for a labourer's " backrto-back " cottage, is probably 4s. per week. Four shillings per week for rent, added to 13s. 4d. for food, makes an expenditure of 17s. 4d. Out of the sovereign there would thus remain a surplus of 2s. Bd. for clothing and all other

uxiiries.

25 PER CENT. INCOME TAX ON THE POOR. The Bd. cf Lod taxation would therefore mean a 25 per cent. Income-Tax upon that small surplus for luxuries of 2s. Bd. weekly. What would be said of any Government which proposed to charge an Income- Tax of 54. in the .£1 upon as much of each and every family's income as remained after paying house-rent and buying the most absolutely necessary fpod ? Could any British Government maintain a p Icy so oppressive by any means excepting military force ? Tariff Reformers, however, argue that the worker could get compensation for the taxes on bread and meat by the lowering of the tobacco-tax and the removal of the taxes on -sugar and tea. Also they maintain that he could get fuller employment and higher wages. A WILL-W-THE -WISP COMPENSATION.

But the removal of the taxes on tea and sugar could not come near to compensating for the taxation of bread and meat. What good would cheap tobacco be to women and children, at any rate, or even to men, at the cost of dearer milk, bread and meat? _

As regards employment and wages, the French and German workers have lower wages and longer hours; and even in the United States, notwithstanding ihe vast natural resources of that country, the workers' hours are longer, and the cost of living is nearly equal to the higher wages. Indeed, it is clear, on reflection, that the thousands of labouring families whose food is taxed will have. so much the less to spend on clothing, so that there must be less employment for spinners, weavers, tailors, •dressmakers, and all the traders who sell these commodities. WHO CAN SEPARATE RAW MATERIALS?

Furthermore, Tariff Reformers are very seriously divided in opinion as to the imposing of Tariff taxes on the raw materials used in our manufactures. What to some men are finished articles are to others raw materials. Some of them describe leather as a raw material which ought to be admitted free ;- others call it a manufactured article which must be taxed thus raising the price of boots and shoes. Again, some Tariff Reformers declare yarn, whether cotton or wool, to be a raw material; others call it a semi-manu- factured article, which should be taxed.

Obviously these definitions depend entirely upon tile different points of view of different workers. A weavet regards woven cloth as a manufactured article. A tailor ov a dressmaker regards cloth as a raw material, and knows that in proportion to the amount of the tax upon it there will be less demand for clothing. TAKE THE CASE OF TIMBER.

One of the most important raw materals imported into this country is timber for building and wood for furniture and other manufactures. In the year 1908 the value of the timber imported by us was £23,037,000. It furnished employment for scores of thousands of carpenters, joiners, and others. Many Tariff Reformers are in favour of taxing imported timber by way of encouraging the home-grown commodity. The quantity of the home-grown wood, however, is comparatively so small that such encouragement could not be worth the cost. On the other hand, every sovereign of revenue raised by a Tariff tax on imported timber must mean so much less employment for carpenters and joiners and for the building trade generally. TARIFF REFORMERS NOT TRUSTED.

We must not trust at all in the promises of Tariff Reformers as to which commodities they will tax and as to how much the 'taxes are to be. We see in other countries that the appetite for Tariff taxation grows without limit, like the appetite for alcoholic drinks. Every time the duties are increased the employers and workers complain that the cost of living and the expenses _a working have still further increased, and that they must have higher crudes. The great majority of Tariff Reformers are interested in the manufacturing industries. These inlustries, they urge, are the most important of all industries and the most remunerative to the employers and the employed engaged therein. The only reason why those interested. in manufactures propose to tax imported food commodities is- that they could not otherwise get the consent of agri- culturists to the taxation of imported manufactures ; because, though our manufacturing communities are apt to forget the fact, the agricultural interest is still more strongly represented in Parliament than any other.

The • whole Tariff Reform agitation is thus, in effect, an brganislitisn of bargaining between various selfish interests, in which those interests agree to pro- tect And bribe each other at the expense of the tax- paying conimunity. The waste is very great ; because the Tariff-Protected: interests are deprived of the healthy influence of a productive competition; and they are encouraged to help themselves and each other at the common expense, instead of becoming self-reliant upon their own skill and industry as producers

In this series of specially written articles, we purpose answering the varieui argumeats used by advocates of Tariff Reform.

A CRTMEAN AND INDIAN MUTINY VETERAN.

On-Saturday the funeral took place at Chester, with military honours, of Private George Dunn, eighty-seven years of age, a Crimean and Indian Mutiny veteran, who resided at 8, Parker's-buildings, Foregate-street. Deceased joined the 82nd Foot Regiment in 1842. and was discharged in i 863, since when he had been in receipt of a pension. He was decorated with the Turkish Methl, Sevastopol (clasp), Indian Mutiny (rin dal), and the Balaclava (clasp), together with his Crimean medal and the medal fo. long service and good conduct. He took part in the battles of Inkerman and Balaclava and the relief of Cawnpore.

PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

FOODS AND FEEDING.

If asked what most tended to failure in poultry farming I should, without hesitation, say injudicious feeding. The majortty of people who keep poultry do so without ever having studied the principal details of their management. I do not know whether the " cruelty Act "includes the protection of poultry or not, but; seeing that after all a fowl is a live creature with feeling, and is domesticated and utilised for the benefit of man, it has a perfect right to be thus pro- tected. Certain observations force one to speak out on this question. Quite recently I have noticed (especially during the long spell of wet weather) poultry Confined in cramped areas, trampling about up to their hocks, in Mud, and being fed in the same condition. Corn simply thrown down to be scrambled for by the half-starved creatures, consequently their crops contain not only the corn but also an equal pro- portion of mud. I wonder how it is possible to keep poultry free from disease under such conditions? And then, blindly ignorant of what is really neces-. sary, such people give up keeping poultry because they say it is not profitable. When I -have seen poultry kept in such a filthy state I have always felt like recommending their owners to start pig-farming, for the hen at least does belong to a more aristocratic community than the pig, but even so, with all the natural tendencies of the pig for 0144 does get its food from a clean trough. The sooner the British poultry farmer begins to realize that cleanliness and proper methods of feeding are necessary that much sooner will he find that poultry can be made to pay. Supposing that a herd of Mikh cows had their food thrown down on the farm-yard and were allowed to gobble it up in the order of th e"Surviva lof the fittest." It is no conundrum to conclude what would happen. The farmer would be minus the rent when the day came ronnd, not to speak of the disease that would follow and ruin his stock. It may seem folly to write in this strain, but these conditions are allowed to continue in many cases as far as the poultry are con- cerned. Therefore let us see if a better state of things cannot he arrived at as we proceed to consider our subject.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
FEEDING FOR TABLE.

It is not my intention to deal with the feeding or fattening of poultry for the market in this article, because such a process can be better dealt with con- clusively at some future date. It is a special branch of industry, and one could not do justice to it in a paragraph. I want rather to give general instructions on the feeding of poultry for general purposes.

FOODS AND THEIR VALUE,

Nitrogenous or albuminous matter is essential for the formation of flesh and bone. Carbonaceous foods are necessary hi supplying internal fat. A common error is that the different foods and the elements they contain are hardly ever considered by the majority of poultry keepers. So we shall take a list of different food stuffs and there relative values. Beans and peas, oatmeal, middlings or thirds,and oats, contain more nitrogen than other grain, and are not only valuable but essential for the formation of flesh and bone. Buckwheat, maize or Indian corn, and hempseed, contain a large percentage of •oil and are very necessary if judiciously given for supplying the fowl with sufficient internal fat to regulate internal heat. I have often deplored the fact that with a great many people maize is used, almost as a staple diet. Consequently while the fowls may look the picture of health you get no eggs because of the predominance of internal fat with a minimum of flesh and bone. I should recommend for a. staple diet in grain good sound oats, as they contain a more even balance of nitrogen and carbon than other grains and if the weather is severe (and especially during the moulting period when more oil is needed) give an extra few meals per week of maize, but you must avoid regular feeding on the latter. I advise these two because I know that it is not always possible to procure a supply of all that have been mentioned.

SOFT FOODS,

Where only a few fowls are kept Biscuit Meal will be found a very handy and useful diet for the morning feed, if a good sample is used. But where a large stock is kept it may be found rather expensive; so I should recommend the following as a good mixture containing all the elements necessary for egg pro- duction and healthy stock :—Two parts Pea-meal, one part Broad-Bran, well scalded with boiling water and then mixed into a crumbly state with Thirds ; any scraps from the table may be added, also a swede turnip that has been well boiled. A good big handful per bird will be the right quantity to give and see that what is not eaten up is taken away. It is a bad plan to leave food lying about as it gets tainted and sour, and if eaten by your fowls in such a condition is very harmful.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
GREEN FOOD.

Cabbage or cauliflower leaves are good, but if you have any difficulty in getting these a turnip or mangold cut in halves (as recommended in the query column a short time ago) will be found to be a good substitute. Clover is now extensively used by some iof our best .poultry farmers, more so n a dry state, and when chopped and scalded together with the soft food • is a very valuabte ingredient, being a highly nitrogenous food. Of course where poultry have a free range on grass you need not. worry about green food, but when kept in Confinement and even on-graSs runs of a limited area green food is absolutely necessary.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
ANIMAL FOOD.

Again, birds on a free 'rang.e will also get their requirement of animal food by picking up worms etC., and as we have many times noticed that fowls always prefer a "grub to a grain." this should be a lesson sufficient .to teach us how necessary it is to supply fowls in confined runs with something equivalent. It is not to be expected that poultry keepers in a small way will be able to afford a green boi4e mill. This is a machine for grinding beef bones. But where such a machine can 'be afforded the cost would soon be repaid, as cut green bone is the most valuable form oi animal food extant. On the other hand when this cannot be done I should recommend my readers to purchase a few pounds of lean cheap meat per week; get it as near all lean as possible and boil it. who, boiled cut it up in small pieces. and give about an ounce per bird at least twice a week. You may use the water it has been boiled in for 'mixing the soft food. If you will see that your fowls have a regular supply of animal food in some form, together with the proper feeding in other directions as already pointed out, you are bound to get eggs,. but you cannot expect a good egg supply when you do not give your fowls the necessary food for making the egg.

CHANGING THE DIET.

Poultry, like all other creatures, appreciate a change of diet, and from the long list of food stuffs mentioned you should have no difficulty in this direction. The more pains you take for the comfort and well-being of your stock that much better will the results be. Ido not wish to infer that you should pamper them, but make their diet appetising. One thing I have °Mated and which i 5 very necessary is to season the soft food with salt according to the quantity, and always have a good supply of fresh clean water to which may be added once a week (especially at this season of the year) a little Epsom salts. Sulphate of Iron is a vain- ableitonic and may be given in the drinking water, a piece the f7rize of a hazel nut to every quart of water. This will very materially help to keep your birds healthy and active.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
GRIT.

It has been pointed out before that grit is necessary for the fowls to grind their food with. A regular supply of sharp flint grit and ground oyster shell should be kept in a small box or trough under cover. Thu can purchase good samples ready prepared and would advise you do so in preference to breaking up your own. - It is not only more 'convenient, but it is prepared in a better condition than if yOu attempted .to produce it,

METIIODS OP FEEDING,

AlwaYs place the soft food either On a clean board or in a trough. I prefer the latter if made narrow so that the fowls cannot get on the food - with their feet. Give the morning food as early as convenient, remembering that of all living creatures it is the fowls who carry out the old maxim "Early to bed and, early to rise." So that to feed as near to their natural cOndition as possible it is essential that you should give them their first meal,early. And while it i§ the "bird who gets the early worm,' it will be the poultry farmer who gets the egg basket full if these few hints are carried out. Make them search for the grain by throwing it down in the litter on the scratching shed floor, and give the last feed as near to roosting time as can be judged by the habits of your fotvls. Next week I ;hall write an article on seasonable hints.

Gentlemen,—Recognizing as I do the gravity of the sitnation which has arisen and of the important issues at stake, I wish to make my position perfectly plain to every member of this Committee. I have held a certain offieial position in this town for nearly twenty years-. I have never had the slightest reason to doubt that as long as Idiscbarged the duties of that position efficiently I had a perfect right to hold it until circum- stances or my own wish led me to reign it. The con- &Vous of my home life, the education of my children, and all future liabilities have been based on this hitherto unquestioned right. I take it, therefore, that, no member of this committee will be surprised to learn that as long as those who have intimate kn ledge of my educational work are perfectly satisfied with it, I can only recognize the law of this land as the authority to whose decree I shall cheerfully bow if called upon to resign it. On the other hand, I take it that you as a Committee are convinced that you are acting in the best interests of the children and the educational progress of this town in giving me notice of dismissal. Although for my own sake and for the sake of the teaching profession lam bound to dispute its legelity, I wish emphatically to sta..° that I do so in no spirit of bitterness and, whatever the result may be, I most earnestly trust that the happy relations which have hitherto prevailed between the Education Com- mittee and the teachers working in the schools of Warrington will remain undisturbed."

Many people received their wages on Monday night and it was therefore thought advisable to make the change. On this exploration the amendment was withdrawn and the minutes were passed.

NORTHERN UNION CUP.

WAKEFIELD WIN TIIE FINAL.

After the long period of 22 years, the %Wakefield Trinity team are again in possession of the chief trophy of Northerd Rugby football. On Saturday, on the Headingley ground, at Leeds, they met and vanquished Hull in the final tie for the Northern Union Cup, scoring no fewer than 17 points to nil after elle of the best finals seen since the competition was established. There was a very large crowd—composed almost entirely of York- shire people—but it did not constitute a record. The record gate assemble4i when Halifax and Salford met in the Cup final at Headingley in 1903, when the attend- ance was 32,860. On Saturday 23,587 persons pi.ssed the turnstiles. The number present, however, was actually much more than this figure. Half-an-hour before the start the press near the bowling green entrance was so great that the gates were broken down and a thousand or more spectators got in—many without paying at all. As, it was, the match receipts came to £1,489 15s. 3d. During the night and elrly morning rain had fallen smartly in the Leeds district, making the ground very soft and treacherous, but the football was of good quality throughout. Teams : Wakefield Trinity—J. Metcalfe, Lack ; E. W. Bennett, W. Lynch, E. Sidwell, and W. G. Simpson, three-quarter backs; H. Slater (captain) and T. H. Newbould, half Lacks ; A. K. Crosland, J. Autcn, U. Taylor, J. Walton, H. Kershaw, and H. Beaumont, forwards. Hull--Taylor, back ; 'Dechan, Connell, Cotteiell end E. Rogers, three-quarter backs ; Anderson and Wallace, half backs ; Boy len, Herridge, Britton, Carroll, Holder, and Havelock, forwards. Referee, Mr. E. H. Smirk, Wigan. Touch judges, Messrs. W. W. Taylor, ilunslet, and W. Emmett, Leeds. From the moment the ball was set in motion Wake- field showed they were determined to "make geod." Their tremendous aggressiveness simply Swept Hull off their feet, and not seven minutes had elapsed before a try was recorded. The Hull tacks could not hold the epposing forwards and the inefficiency of the three- quarter lire in parsing and tackling wits most marked. Their forwards, tco, lacked the requisite turn of speed, while the halves were a good way inferior to the Triiiity two. From the scrums the leather nearly always went to the winners, whose tackling, trickiness and grit were worthy of all praise. The tries were obtained by Bennett (2). Newbould, Crosslend, and Simpson, others especially worthy of recognition being Lyuch and Slater. On the losing side Dechan nas nearly success- ful with a 6ne tun on one oc.easion, and Taylor's oik was the redeeming feature of Hull's fine display. Final: Wakefield. 1 goal, 5 tries, 17 points ; Hull, nil.

Semi-Finals.

RYLANDS BROS. No. 1 v. P. & E. No. 2.

Played on Saturday. Play had only been in progress a few minutes when a heavy storm commenced, and the game was stopped for some time. %Then the players re-entered the field, water practically covered the ground. The match was, nevertheless, very keeply fought, and P. & K. soon had the lead through Hanoi) scoring a try, which was improved by Spencer. Just before half-time Broadhurst scored for Rylands. Giblz failed at goal. lialf-time :—P. ev, K. 5, R3ll.nds 3. Resuming the game became very rough, and Lee, P. rS; K.'s h avy forward, was ordered oft for fighting. WIGAN "A" SUSPENDED FOR. TWO YEMIS. An important mef,ting of the Loncishire County Union was held in Manchester on Monday evening, Mr. J. U. Smith (Widnes), presiding. The act ioi of the Wigan club in failing to play in the Shield fit.al at Salford on Monday' the day selected, was considered. It wr.s decided to declare the Oldham "A" team the IT:nners of the Shield and mm dal. It was further decided that the Wigan club be not allowed to take part in the Lancashire Sh:eld Competition till a pegtod of two 3ears has expired.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL

OUGHTRINGTON ST. PETERS V. CRANFOTID LADS.— This final tie in the Medal Competition was decided on the Pepper-street ground, Lymm, on Saturday last. A heavy downpour of rain on Friday night made the "going "Oeavy and a contituation of the rain at intervals on Saturday did not. improve matters in this respect. It did not, however, prove too much for the enthusiasm of the supporters of both teams, there being a good number present. St. Peter's were first. to attack but wtre driven back by Conford. Free- kicks on both sides came to nothing. Cranford next attacked strongly. Bell having a warm handful to deal with' tbe following corner being unavailing. St. Peter's left wing now had a turn, Simpson finally sending the ball across to the opp, s:to wing where 11. Holt made no escape with his oppor- tunity. The torrential rain now proved too much and a truce was called shortly after which hslf-time came with the score St. Peter's 1, Cranford nil. Mid-field play saw the opening of the second half, Shaw and Kay put. ing in some good work for the home team. The visitors' centre receiving the ball from a goal kick by Woods, sf nt in a well-directed shot which the latter in trying to clear mis-kick, the ball striking the post and rebounding into the net. From a nice pass Simpson placed his side ahead. St. Peter's now held the whip hand; and only occasionally did the visitors get out of their own half. Result :—St. Peter's 2, Cranford Lads,l.

WARRINGTON FOOTBALL

CLUB.

REVIEW OF SEASON 1933-9.

By " Ovalist."

To-day sees the wind-up of another football season the fourteenth since the formation of the Northern rnion. Time is marching on, and the Union has now in established position in the North and a promising foothold in South .Wales, and New South Wales, Australia. Early troubles have been overcome, good management has succeeded speculative experiments which at ono time 'threatened the existence of the pro- fessional game, and to-day the Northern Union can claim to be a power in the land, its influence is increasing as the quality of the football improves. New friends are being made, and the public backing is such. that fears of a general inroad by the Associa- tion game can now be treated lightly. Professional Rugby Football has come to tay, and in my opinion it is the finest winter game extant either for players or public. It is only a question of time—perhaps a fairly long time—before Professional Rugby takes precedence of Amateur Rugby in this country. When that day dawns then, and net before, can, England hope to regain her lost laurels in the gnine. While the Union has advanced and waxed strong what has the Warrington Club been doing? The answer is easy, and satisfactory. Warrington F.C. twas never so strong as it is to-day, and this fact alone is an eloquent tribute to the management of those men who have recently been subject to severe criticism.. I am not suggesting that the cri,icismwas ='•- altogether unmerited, G. DICKENSON but it is desirable The daptain who has secured h l f International honours this season. tatl!l e a ILI at -sf should a t least look Ideoorks a little beyond their noses and give credit where it is clearly due. The Warrington Club has certainly not been a mismanaged organization, or it would not stand so high in the football world as it does to-day. Where the fault does lie, is in the fact that with a powerful teani and considerable financial resource, the past two seasons have been barren of achievement. Why ? is a.per- anent question that is not easy to answer. Take the seasonjust concluded.. Warrington have never been more capably represented on the'. field -• of play, • yet they have failed to :secure any one .of the trophies offered to the seniors, viz. : The Leanne Championship, ' the N.U. Cup, the Lancashire Cup- and the South West Lancashire Cup. True, the "A " teaw .have done themselves-and. the Club credit in winning once more the Combination championship, but even they disappointed their supporters when they failed to annex also the Lancashire Junior Shield with an exceptional opportuntty before them. The First Team made the serious error of over- looking the fa.ct that a good Start is half the battle in the League. They lost their first three matches in succession, and out of the first nine League engage- ments only obtained five points. This WES a deplor- able start to the season's campaign and spread consternation through the ranks of the Club's supporters. Whether it could have been prevented is an open question, but it certainly seemed that there wavi want of early training, and the absence for so long a period of forwards like Shugars and Thomas was never satisfactorily explained. These con-. tributory causes placed a Landicap on the team which they could never get rid of, and though there was some brilliant football in the latter half of the season the chance of getting in the first .four of the _League was irretrievably lost during. the opening month. Warrington's position in the League at the close of the campaign is conse- quently rather worse than it was -a year ago, when they finished seventh against tenth to-day. In the Lancashire Cup Competition Warringtbw made a creditable show, considering that they were drawn away in each round, and only succumbed at Wigan by 15 jpoints to 10 points.

When the Northern Uniott Cup Competi-

hopes ran'high. The

team was strong in every department and

playing:with confid-

e- nee and skill. Surely the early failures

would be atoned for. But no, the first round

Warrington's

hopes daslied the ground by Leeds, and at Wilderspool! The occasion is too recent to need recall-

ing in detail, but the bitterness of the J. FISH, • whose scoring figures this swon defeat has not yO are a record for the Club. passed away, and .. _

there are many burning questions to be asked at the annual meeting of the Club concerning the absence of such men as Fish, Dickenson, and Brooks on that eventful and unhappy day. Warrington's failure was-all the more aggravat- ing when the team scihtillated with brilliant footlxiltin succeeding matches and put on 224 points to 53 at the expenae of subsequent opponents. But for that unfortunate slip, the hopes of a triumphant Cup contest might easily have materialised, for there was certainly no superior side left in the competition. I said at the time that the disaster taught One or two lessons in tetun management which were needed and which will probably never be forgotten, and I am still of that opinion. The best features of the season's football have been the sequences of successes which the team registered once it had found its feet. From October 31st to November 28th, they won six tames off the reel, from December 19th to January 2nd, five games were won in succes- sion, and from March 13th to April Bth, there was another run of five victories. In the whole of their League career Warrington have never shown form equal to this, and it proves conclusively that the Club at present possesses the finest team which has ever donned the primrose and blue. Among the many individual performances of note, the greatest was the victory by 27 points to none over the hitherto unbeaten Oldham team. In that engagement Warrington touched the highwater mark of scientific football and gave a display which could not have been excelled by any combination playing the game. In the last home lengagement Warrington reproduced much Of this brilliance against a weaker side—St. Helens—with the result that the huge score of 9 goals, .20 tries, 78 points was compiled, creating a''record not only for the Club but for The Northern Union. The visit of the Australian team aroused'i considerable amount of interest in the Union, atid though the Kangaroos did not display the genius of the earlier tourists from New Zealand, they played fine football. Warrington was one of the few clubs which played them twice, winning the first game by 10 points to 3, and drawing the second with a score of 8 points each.

the very forefront in the Northern Union as a half- back, and has been picked in every international and Lancashire county match and alwAys acquitted him- self in international form. Had there been a visit. to Australia this summer, he would have been among the first to have been chosen for the tour. Dickenson has also gained international and County honours, has captained the Warrington team throughout the season, and has played in magnificent form at left centre. , This has been his best. season. Fish has not been picked for the representative matches, but he has passed all records as a club scorer, and once more heads the list of scorers for the club by a large majority. He has scored very nearly one third of the total • scores of the Warrington team. In fact he and Thomas have very ,nearly scored one half of Warrington's points. Thomas again has also good cause to be proud of the season's work. He has played very little more than half the season, and yet he comes second in the list of scorers with 112 points. ,On Easter Monday ho scored 33 points against St. Helens, and this beats the previous individual record for one match which was held by Fish, who on October 20, 1906, scored 30 points against Huddersfield. He also equalled Fish's record of kicking nine goals in one match, and also equalled that player's record of scoring five tries in one match. For a forkvard his feat was a remarkable one. Taylor and Tilley, the two Widnes men, have again played consistently good football, while Jenkins and Lloyd have worked the scrum well for Brooks. Bradshaw and Treharne have done well on the right wing, and the younger player gives promise of making a name for himself. Summer training should give him just the extra turn of speed which is so essential. O'Neill has been a great acquisition in the forward ranks, and it is safe to say that his skilful work in the open has been an eye-opener to the Warrington -men. Boardman, Shugars and Dowell have been prominent forwards, and among the younger men Chesters, Bradshaw, and P. Boardman are rapidly approaching first-class. The weaknesses of the team have been few but unmistakable. It is only recently that the right wing position hat been filled with any great. success, and much depends upon Bradshaw for the future. The centre positions are safe in the present. hands,' but there is no reserve of any class, and it behoves the management. to secure another good centre at the earliest opportunity. Another good half is also ' required, and unless Tilley chooses to play another season the full, back's position will be one of con- siderable anxit;ty. The aid) cannot afford to rest on its oars for a moment, and no opportunity must be missed of securing capable men in all positions, forl the simple reason that among the local juniors there are very few who give any evidence of training on. They are Very good second second class men, but they are only second class.

CYCLING

WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB.

The run to Acton Brid,o wr,s-carried out on Saturday last under very trying weither conditions. Ita'n in plenty and muddy roads did not tend to enhance the pleasures of cycling. A respectable number of members however turned cut to brave the elements, and the destination was reached by easy stages, and after frequent stops for shelter from the " beautiful" April showers. Howling was again, as on the previous Satur- day run, not available, and resort had to be made to recreation which was permissible under tLe adverse circumstances. Admirers of nature turned their atten- tion to country lanes, while other members confined .themselves to the amusement which cover offers. After tea acc ncert sas given; and owing to the absence of the first team, the songs were rendered by the second team rnemLers in a very excellent manner. An e rrly start home was made, and Warrington reached after a wet but successful ride.

MOTOR CAR FATALITY. No Blame Attached to the Driver.

An inquest was held by Mr. Coroner Brighouse at Wet rington, on Monday, on William John Coley, aged nine years, son of William Thomas Coley, labourer, 99, Plumpton-street, vtho was knocked down by a motor car, owned by Mr. J. G. Withinshaw, of Penketh, the previous Wednesday aftkrnoon and died in the evening. —Mr. F. R. Roberts represented Mr. Withinshaw. • Deceased's father said his son was sent on an errand, and in doing so he bad to cross Liverpool-road. Soon after his son had gone a neighbour came to him and said he MIR wanted in Liverpool-road. Witness saw a boy had been injured by a motor car, but did not know it was his ' son until be had a ]ook at him. The boy was unconscious and bleeding from a wound at the back of the brad. He pits placed in the motor car and con- veyed to the Infirmary, where he died at 9.30 the same night. In answer to the Coroner, witness said the chauffeur was sober. He beard from the people by the car that the toy was crossing the I oad and that they shouted to him to get out of the way, but he seemed confused. The boy had good sight and hearing and was smart, everybody giving him a good name. The Co.oner : Have you heard anything front any- body that would lead you to thiak that the driver of the motor car was to blame? Witness : No, sir. Mary Bond, wife of Richard Bond, labourer, 232, Liverpool-road, was then called and said that she saw the motor car going in the direction of Sankey and saw Coley going across the road. When the boy left the focopath the car was close upon him and when he got in the middle of the road he appeared to tura round and was knocked. down.

Replying to the Coroner,witness said the motor car was going very slowly. She bad never seen one go so.slowly. James Ilarding, 24G, Liverpool-ioad, said he saw the csr approtfch the boy. The chauffeur sounded his horn. Coley seemed bewildered as to which way to go and the car struck him en the left side and the ironwork under the car caught the left side of his head. 141)13 ing to questions, witncs said the car was going at about the sAne speed as the tram cars, and the boy met with his death in a purely accidental way. The chauffeur had neither time to pull up nor to go to the left or right of the boy, in fact he had not a chance. George West, chauffeur, employed by Mr. WI thinshaw, said he gave up cab driving five weeks ago and ieceived a forunLhts training by lessrs.Bellian and Davics, of ‘Var.ingt.,n, as a motor di leer. He entered the service of Mr. Withinshaw and to, k out a driver's licence on the 3rd inst. .

Thu Coroner: Have you passed any examinetion -- •

_ Witness : No, sir. _ The Cotoner: Simply pplied for sour licence?

Witeess: Yes. Continuing, witness raid he had driven the car about the town and in the district. On the occasion of the accident the car was going at-between eight and ten miles.an hour. :Near Barnard-street the boy Coley stepped off the footpath. The car was only three or four yards from him and it was impossible tor witness to pull up. He put both the hand brake and the foot b:ake on and stopped one length of the car past the boy. In answer to the Coroner, witness said the boy seen-x(1 to iun off the footpath and then stand still in front of the car. It was a 30-horse power car. By Mr. Roberts: Witness had no need to hurry home. He had instructions from Mr. Withinshaw to go along Liverpool-road very carefully. The Coroner: I suppose 3ou found your occupation as a cabearn had gone, and, like most of the younger men, caarfined your attention to motor cars? Witness: Yes sir. C.,ntinuing witness said that he had been in the service of Mr. Connor, and previously was engaged by Sir Gilbert Greenall. Mr. Withinshaw said that he laiew West as a cab- man. Witness sent him to Messrs. Bellian and Davies to be trained as a motor driver, end was told that be could drive properly, and be (Mr. Withinshaw) was satisfied that he could do so. Wert was a careful driver and never exceeded 15 miles an hour. Witness had specially warned him about Lkerpool-road which was a perfect death-trap,. as children were in the habit of running out of entries. The Coroner. —They come out like shots from a gun. The Coroner turning to the jury said he bad nothing( to say against the motor car. It was a mode of loco- motion that was not only luxurious, but it was a pastime for the rich. On the other band it was also a corn:eerier(' mode of locomotion that was extremely pseful and it enabled a great number of people to he employed. It was an industry that ought to be sup- ported instead of anything being said against, it. It was quite useless to say a car must not travel at the rate of more than 15 or 20 miles an hour. Everything dep. tided on the skill and care of the man at the steer- ing wheel. He himself was not rich enough to own a motor car, but like a sensible person, be rode iu his friends' c.,irs, lie had been a good many journeys and was perfectly certain the Loeislature neert not fix any particular speel. Ho agreed with Mr. Withinsbaw that it was a veiitable death-trap. The jury returned a Nerdicf of" Accidental death," and said in their opinion no blame `was attached to the driver. They expressed their sympathy with the bereaved parents.. Mr. Roberts expressed the profonnd sorrow which Mr. Withinshnw felt at the occurrence. Of cour-e, nothing could equal the sorrow of the parents, but the mental disturbance suffered by Mr. Withinsiaw since hearing that it was his car bad certainly been very great. It was only right to ray that Mr. Withinshaw had done all he could to help the parents in their grief, both financially and otheraise. The Coroner said he agreed w:th the verdict of the jury and their expression of opinion. Wiett he was going to eery did sot reflect on West in any shape or form, but be was strongly of opinion that a statutory rate of speed was absolutely useless from the stand- point of the public—this case exemplified that—aad he protested most strongly against licences being issued, to persons without a prelitninary examination. Lawyers and doctors were not allowed to practise until they had passed examinations, and men were not allowed to drive rei.way engines without' passing examinations as to physical finless, eyesight, &c., and it was time the Legislature stepped in here and prevented the indis- criminate issue ot licences, He was told by a gentle- man in the court and the Chief Constable that a licence had actually be, n issued to a blind man to drive a motor car. (Laughtrr). The Chief Constable.—That is not in Warringten, Mr. Coroner. (Laughter). The Coroner.-1 know; you would be on his track at once.

THE BUDGET, unusually e-xtens iv e

speech which he found ‘" Physically exhausting that the House adjourned in order to give him an interval of rest, Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE unfolded a Budget 'Bingularly complex in character and making - 'Several important new departures alike in the /4,nethods of levying taxation and in the area " to use the cant word of the day, frol/1 which the National Revenue is drawn,. Great indeed is the area for discussion opened up by the proposals made 'to Parliament on Thursday but scarcely one proposal in the 'series can be condemned on any ground as inadvisable and not even one can be justly characterised as vindictive or unfair.

'Some of the London MARRIED WOMEN papers have com- TEACHES. mented upon the War- rin gt on Education Committee's determination to dispense with the services of the married women teachers, and none that we know of have expressed an adverse view. It is recognised, as the "Westminster Gazette" points out, that the further engagement of married teachers requires consideration in view of the fact that there are so many qualified women unable to obtain posts, and that to the num- ber of unemployed 4,000 will be added in July, when the colleges release their finished pupils. In Warrington the mutinous spirit still prevails among the married teachers concerned, though the Education Committee has confirmed the Sub-committee's decision and sent out the notices ter- minating engagements three months from to-day. There are rumours of a test case in law being taken to decide whether marriage is an "educational ground" justifying dis- missal, but we should hardly think the point worth contesting seriously. There is no room for doubt that the day of the married teacher is over. With' the march of the times and the pressure of competition, she must " willy nilly " retire to the kitchen or join the ranks of the suffragettes.

Gratifying progress was • THE BRIDGES. made at the special meeting of the Council on Tuesday in the momentous new bridges question. There was a good deal of repetition of well worn arguments, but in the end it was decided that Warrington bridge shOuld be built of ferro-concrete, and that'estimates should be obtained for structures 70ft. and 80ft. wide. There is still a wide cleavage of opinion on the question of width, and Coun- cillor Pemberton has given notice of a resolution for the Council meeting next Tuesday to authorise the obtaining of tenders for a 60 feet bridge. We certainly ought to have the comparative costs of 60 feet and 80 feet, particularly as an influential section of the Council has come round to the conviction that the maximum width is the best. Con- sidering the short length, 120 best, a width of 80 feet seems to us extravagant even in view of the large demands on space which we are told will be made by posterity. For all we know to-day, posterity may prefer air-space to road:surfaces. In any case they could get along comfortable with either a 60 or 70 feet bridge over the Mersey. At last the site of the Howley Foot-bridge has been settled. We believe the position now chosen will give general satisfaction, for it provides a via media between the extreme schemes which were previously on the tapis. Residents on both sides of the river will secure means of inter-communication without being involved in a round-about journey. The scheme should now be pushed on -Without delay. - We ought to have the bridge in use before the summer and the delights of Victoria Park are passed away.

We are pleased. to. see HOWARD PLACE. that Alderman Bennett has no intention of abandoning the struggle to prevent the desecration of Howard Place by the erection of a public convenience in the centre. Our readers will probably recollect that at the April meeting of the Town Council the -resolution to perpetrate such an outrage on good taste was only carried by 15 votes to 14, and against the expressed and vigorous protests of all the shopkeepers and residents in the neighbourhood. Even on sanitary grounds there is no justification for the pro- posal, end we hope the Council will demon- strate its recovery of a sense of the fitness of things on Tuesday by carrying Alderman Bennett's proposition to rescind the resolution of a month ago.

The new Baron Eficerton RIGHTS AT of Tatton has earned ROSTHERNE. unenviable notoriety by the issue •of an edict forbidding his tenants at Rostherne, and some of the neighbouring Cheshire hamlets, to cater for visitors upon pain of being compelled to leave their homes. Every cyclist, pedestrian, and picnicker for fifty miles round is now holding the name of Egerton in abhorrence. The arrogance of these lords of the land in free England is at times too much for the stomach of the proud Britisher. There are signs of it disagreeing so strongly with the public digestion in this latest instance that the bold bad Baron may deem it advisable . ere long to recant. We know one charming old lady in rustic Rostherne who provides delectable teas for privileged visitors like ourselves which one would n.ot forego even on pain of excommunication or boiling oil. Nowhere is there such home-made bread, such golden butter or such spotless table-linen. We shall tempt her to defy, cheat, or defeat the crass purposes of the lord of the manor. Who is he, forsooth, that dare step in between ourselves and these homely delights? We know him not except as an individual who probably hates his social " inferiors " as he does the Socialists. Foolish man! Agitators will multiply by the hundred-fold when tyranny such as this stalks abroad naked and unashamed.

Although the Post THE POST OFFICE Office revenue for last ' RECORD. year showed a want of elasticity in sympathy with the general state of trade, Mr. Sydney Biixton had a remarkable record of progress to lay before the House of Commons. TIQR cheaper rate of magazine postage to Canada has produced a strikink increase in the num- ber of British magazines and catalogues sent to the Dominion. Adopting the phrase of Swift, extolling the man who made two blades of grass grow where one grew before as one of the greatest benefactors of humanity, Mr. Buxton said he was content to go down to posterity as the man who made two British magazines read in Canada instead of one American magazine.' Over 4200 cash-on-delivery parcels went from Great gritain to Canada during the past year. which means that the Post Office has been giving a real Preference, not only to the British producer; butiz to-the Canadian con- , .?, sinner. - The institution of a penny post to the United States is an achievement of which

the Postmaster-General may well be proud. It is true that so far it has cost £130,000 a year, but the volume of correspondence is increasing. Like the institution of the Penny Post in the United Kingdom, such a cheapening of rates is certain eventually not only to add to the amenities of life; but to bring in a revenue which eventually will far more than compensate for the temporary sacrifice of profit. As Mr. Buxton said, 'Bread' cast in this way upon the waters will come back before many days— and with butter too.'

OUR MISCELLANV.

Liberals and Social Reformers generally rejoice as much in the Budget as the Hollanders do in their new Princess. Both give bright hopes for the future. * * *

There have not been many lawyer Chan- cellors of the Exchequer. Mr. Lloyd George's immediate predecessor, Mr. Asquith, was one, and Sir William Harcourt was another, but otherwise it would be needful to go back to the time of Mr. Robert Lowe (afterwards Lord Sherbrooke) in 1868 to fins" a previous instance.

Some very good buds have been related in the current number of the " Windsor Magazine." Dr. Macnamara has come along this week with another which should not be lost sight of. \The teachers' champion is reported as saying: "Especially would my ideal system concentrate on the children. Their futures are before them; most of ours are behind us." * *

Hurrah for the Chancellor! If he had done nothing else than introduce the prin- ciple of the taxation of unearned increments due to the artificial enhancement of land value created by the growth of towns, &c. he would have performed a courageous and epoch-making feat. Will the Tories dare to go back on it when their turn comes a few years hence? The "last Free Trade Budget" goes a long way towards knocking the flimsy bottom out of Tariff Reform.

* * * What is said to be the first case of its kind under the Children Act has resulted in an aunt being fined ss. for giving beer to her niece, aged two and a half years. The defendant plead that she did not know this to be prohibited by the Act, and, no doubt, some little time must elapse before the Act is thoroughly understood. The practice of giving alcohol to babies and young children is a thoroughly had and indefensible one, and we trust that the Act may lead to its being put a stop to.

The career of Dr. Clifford,whose ministerial jubilee was commemorated on Tuesday, would have rejoiced the heart of Dr. Smiles. His parents were working-people, and the education he received was of the poorest, as may be gathered from the fact that at eleven he was sent to work in a lace factory as a "threader." This was in the days before the Factory Acts, and Dr. Clifford remembers what it was at times to w-ork twice round the clock. He got on, however, and by sixteen was a manager of one of the depart- ments. * * *

The higliest-distinctions which his Church can confer upon him have been bestowed, upon Dr. Clifford. He has been twice President of the Baptist Union, President of the General Baptist Association, of the London Baptist Associatiori, of the Baptist World Alliance, and of the National Council of Evangelical Free Churches—though these are, of course, only a few of the public positions of one sort and another which he has held from first to last. Of the many great men and famous whom he has known Dr. Clifford speaks most lovingly of Mr. Gladstone.

In point of age Mr. Lloyd George, who is forty-six must be reckoned amongst the youngest of latter-day Chancellors of the ExChequer, though Mr. Austen Chamberlain was only forty when he held the position in '1903, and Lord Randolph Churchill,when he accepted the Chancellor Ship and Leadership of the Commons in 1886, was younger still, being only thirty-seven. Mr. Gladstone was forty-three when he first became Chancellor, Lord St. Aldwyn (Sir M. Hicks) forty-eight, Mr. Childers fifty-five, Sir W. Harcourt fifty- nine, Mr. Ritchie sixty-four, and Mr. Asquith fifty-four. * *

Blackfriars Bridge-, which Alderman Bennett, of Warrington, says is a disgrace to Manchester, has a history which would perhaps interest Miss Maud Allan. It was the immediate successor of a wooden foot- bridge erected in 1761 by a company of actors who, having been refused permission to give performances in Manchester, hired a building in Water-street, Salford, for the purpose, and erected the footbridge for the convenience of their Manchester patrons. Their bridge survived until 1819, when the building of the present bridge was begun.—" Manchester Guardian."

* * There was one passage• in Mr. Austen Chamberlain's speech on the Post Office which is so admirable in its sentiment that we desire for it the widest possible publicity. To have a democratic franchise used to promote particular industries at the e4sense of the community at large was one of the most demoralising and dangerous conditions that could be imagined, and, whether in or out of office, he was willing to co-operate with any who saw their way to _try to avert that danger: _

Austen ought fo join the Free Trade Union, for in his own concise phraseology he indicates accurately and eloquently, the mischief of a Protective tariff. 2.:

Although it is better that the employer should provide his workmen with adequate homes rather than that they should live under bad conditions, yet it is best of all if the employe provides his own house.—Mr. W. H. Lever, M.P.

Many young people are so carried away by Socialistic views that when they find a minister not in sympathy with them they cease their chapel attendance, or go to some Labour Church.—Dr. Marshall, President of the Baptist Union. If the workmen of the country had shown as much interest in the problem of unemploy- ment as they do in the winning of a football cup, the 'question would have been settled long ago.—Mr. D. J. Shackleton, M.P. * * *

Mr. Leslie Harris, the well-known Society entertainer, tells .a good story against him- self. Recently he called in a member of the plumbing craft to do some work at his house in Knotty Ash. The plumber came, half finished the work, and then—as plumbers often do—withdrew for a spell. The delay troubled the humorist, so he wrote a polite and half-bantering letter of protest, whimsi- cally advotating the early completion of a work which had been so well begun. When he had addressed the envelope to Mr. Plumber, it occurred to him to add the,play, ful parenthesis ("in his spare time ",), was why the plumber turneWT:Wiefaaa inquiring whether it was a universal

supposition that plumbers kept a supply of assistants hanging on hooks for the immedi- ate services of impatient customers. But the sting of his retort was on the envelope. He, too, indulged in a playful parenthesis. The address read: "Mr. Leslie Harris, Humorist (I don't think)." * * *

• General satisfaction is felt at the 'prospect of the Board of Trade and the Local Govern- merit Board being raised to a status corres- ponding to their importance. It would be a great improvement if all Government Departments involving administrative duties were raised to the same. level. Prime Ministers would thus be saved from the embarrassment of having to put square pegs into round holes in the distribution of offices, because to offer a politician with claims to high office a Department for which he was specially .qualified by experience and special qualifications, would seem to imply a slight upori his political importance. Mr. Glad- stone's appointment of Mr. Chamberlain to the Board of Trade received an unfortunate inteiTretation, which would not have been possible if the varying scale of emolument had not seemed to mark this department with an absurd inferiority, and it was well known that Mr. Hanbury chafed at his rele- gatibn to the Board of Agriculture. * *

The good people of Lymm may once more breathe freely. The threatened vandalism has been averted, and the ancient Village Cross, of which /they are so justly proud. is not to be disfigured by the telegraph wires. In response to the urgent representations from the inhabitants, the Postmaster- General has intimated that he is willing to remove one of the poles to the other side of the road, and at their meeting on Thursday the Urban District Council gladly sanctioned the chimge. The Postmaster-General in his letter stated that the original position was not decided by •the Post Office engineer arbitrarily but was chosen by him in consul- tation with the representatives of the Urban Council. This authority however, strongly disclaims any responsibility for the choice. Be that as it may not only the villager's but visitors to picturesque tymm from a wide area will be glad that the appeal to the Post- master-General has met with such a satis- factory response.

Warrington Printers at St Helens. A team representing Warrington priaters m.t th,?ir fel!ow craftsmen of St, Ilt•lens on Saturday, and an enjoyable game resulted in a win for the Warrington men by two goals to one. After the match the St. Helens team entertlined the visitors to supper. A 'smoker " followed, songs being rendered by Messrs. Lonsdale, Fell and Wright (Warrington) and Messrs. Hughes,. White and Naylor (St. Helens). Several toasts were submitted and responded to, and the singing of the National Anthem brought an enjoyable evening t 3 a close.

Warrington Man Killed. Walter Hutchinson, a young man belonging to Warrington, but living in lodgings in Partington, mct with a shocking death just before noon on Saturday at the Partington Paper Mills of Messrs. D. and S. Ockleston. • He was engaged in whitewashing one of the rooms when it is supposed he placed his ladder near some revolving machinery. His apron was caught in the shafting, and after being whirled round several times his dead-body fell to the floor terribly mutilated. There was no one near at the time to, render assistance.—The inquest was held on Monday at the Greyhound Inn by Mr. Yates, coroner, and a verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. PUBLIC BENEFIT UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Death of an Old Forester.

Many of our readers, and particularly those associated with Friendly Societies, will regret to hear of the death of Mr. Matthew Lowe, of Church-street. The deceased joined Court " Hope," 431 in the year 1859, and was a regular attender at the meetings of the Order of Foresters. He was laid to rest in the Warrington Cemetery on Sunday last, and the follow- ing brethren assembled to pay him their last tribute of respect :--D.C.R. J. Walsh, P.C.R.,s 'P. Wilding, sen., J. st.—got. "Count Hannibal."

Mr. Oscar Asche and Miss Lily Brayton are appear ing at the Shakespeare Theatre, Liverpool, this week in "Cunt Hannibal" prior to their Australian tear. There has seldom been a more popular week at the handsorce Liverpool Theatre, and crowded houses have - been the rule. "Count Hannibal" is one of those stifling romances, thick with intrigue, fighting and love making, which appeal to nearly all play- goers. It has been cleverly dramatised from the fine novel of the same name by Stanley Weyman, and in the bands of r. Oscar Asehe and his charming wife the Pay is intensely abs)rb- ing: The acting of the whole company is first class, and the scenes are mounted with a sumptuousnas and taste which has rarely been equalled in the provinces. Mr. Asche makes a magnificent Count Hannibal -- ruthless, domineer- ing yet gallant and chivalrous. The study of Charles IX. the vacillating monarch who sanctioned the slaughter of the Huguenots round which the romance is written, is a distinct feather in the cap of Mr. Herbert Greenwood, while high praise must also be accorded Ur. Gerald Kay Souper for his interpre- tation of the faint-hearted lover Tignonville. There are only two women characters of note in the play. Miss Brayton invests -the part of Clothilde, the Lady of Vrillac with rare,cherra, And equally -good in the Jigbter role of Madame? Stt, Lo is Miss Elfridu Clement. The Warrington Male Voice Choir won the first prize in the chief choral competition at the Tyldesley Eisteddfod on Saturday. ,The test piece was "On the Ramparts" and the adjudicators (Hr. W. M. S. .GARLAND, Ironmonger, Bridge st.—Advt. , " Delusive " Trading. _ _

A meeting callei by the St. Helens and Diitrict Grocers and Provision Dealers' Association was held -at the Fleece Hotel, on Tuesday night, to meet Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., with the object of asking him to take up a proposA amendment of the law with regard to trading, but owing to his Parliamentary duties he could not attend. The association had formulated a clause which they suggested should be added to the Lotteries Act or the Prevention of Corruption Act, which would make it an offence to offer, promise, or give to any purchaser of goods any check or coupon for any present; dividend, or bonus. Alderman J. Green, who presided, said they had a lot of evidence to show that pelple did-ndi get Value for their money when they received coupons and the like. There was a good dgal of delusion in the trading of the country, and if some such clause as they were suggesting was made law it would do a great deal to protect the public and to purify the method of trading, Mr. W. H. Owen and Mr. J. Povey, on behalf of the Warrington Association, slid that they were whole-heartedly in sympathy with the movement, and they would be glad t 3 attend a further meeting. The Chairman said that all they could do was to wait for Mr. Seddon to fix another date and then to call a meeting. This was agreed to.

General Sir John FreAch. Inspector-General of the Forces, paid a flying visa to Warrington on Tuesday tn continuation of his tour of inspection of the head- quarters of the various Territorial units in Lanca- shire. The famous General, who played such a dis- tinguished part in the South African campaign, was received at the Drill Hall at one o'clock. by Lieut.-Colonel J. D. Fairclough, commanding the 4th Battalion Prince of Wales's Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) and Captain and 'Adjutant Mott. (commanding the West Lancashire Division Territorial Forces), Brigademajor the Hon. Marjoribanks, Brigadier-General Maxwell, C. 8., Colonel J. A. Edelsten, V.D. "A WORKHOUSE STRIKE." ?,

When a man was sentenced to three months' hard labour at West Ham on Tuesday, for refu..ing to perform his allotted task at the Workhouse, it was stated that there were a number of young men in the institution who had made up their minds not to work, and in con- sequence there was "a workhouse strike."

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

For some time past the subject of old a,-,e pensions has been spoken of inside and out ceParliament. The subject has even got to the Music Halls, but they do not ridicule it, as some have done. They look upon it as a boon. At any rate the following dialogue will explain the position exactly. Says the gentleman " Kirby" to the lady " Kirby " " Will you marry me ?" "No ! you have no money" was the reply. "But I have great expectations said he. " What are they ?" said she. " Five shillings a week at 70" came the answer triumphantly. If ever he does receive his pension it is to be hoped he _will be able to receive it at 65 years of age. These comedy and , burlesque entertainers were about the best,turn of the evening. Their repartee was simply brilliant, and secured much applause. Harwood's Juveniles provide both a pathetic and enjoyable entertainment. The scene portrayed was where the children of well-to-do parents were having a. good time—for it was Christmas Eve—and some poor children were outside in the open, cold and starving.. In their merriment the former did not notice them; but after a while they glanced in their direction an seeing their pitiable plight they invited them in on their promise to take part in their games. This they did, and the cakewalk' was much appreciated. Miss Adelaide Waldrop's illustrated songs were well rendered. Miss Lily Black entertained the audience in a manner deserving of praise. Roma and Romain, high class musicians, fully justified their description, on various instruments. Good jokes are always appreciated and Mr. Chris Baker received much encouragement as he told his stories. Mabel Thorne, the popular comedienne, was ' in a happy mood and divulged many of her secrets to the enjoyment of all present. Raymond's Bio-Tableaux supplied splendid new pictures, and the orchestral selections were well played.

WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF COMMERCE. Bank Quay Station and Warrington Bridge.

At a meeting held at Market-gate Chambers, on Thursday, the President (3lr. Oswald Lofthouse) in the chair, the SECRETARY (Alderman Bennett) said that he had received a letter from the London and North-- Westera Railway Company acknowledging the re3olution of the Council lb reference to the erection of a new station at Bank Quay and stating that the matter would receive careful attention. He added that he had received a similar letter from the Town Clerk acknow- ledging the resolution of the Council in favour of making Warrington Bridge 80 feet wide, and reported that the Carporation had, by a large majority, decided to ask for tenders for a bridge of 70 and 80 feet in width respectively.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
STATE OF THE NAVY.

The SECRETARY also called attention to the meeting which bad recently been held at the Guildhall, under the auspices of the London Chamber of Commerce, to consider the State of the Navy, and, after same dis- cussion, it was proposed by Mr. Holman Kingdom, seconded by Mr. E. James Hall, and unanimously resolved : "That this Council is strongly of opinion that immediate steps should be taken to ensure the adequate protection of our world-wide commerce to safeguard the food supplies of the United Kingdom, and to maintain the unquestioned supremacy of the British Empire upon the sea." A communication had been received from Mr. de F. Pennefather, suggesting a Conference between the Chambers of Commerce of Germany and this country to consider the present serioui naval rivalry between the two nations; but, whilst some approval of the proposal was expressed by members of the Council, others were of opinion that such a Conference would prove futile, and it was felt that the initiative, in a matter of thiq importance, should be taken by the Associated Cbambers or some other largo and influential body.

WARRINGTON PUBLICAN SUMMONED, " CASE DISMISSED: At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday before Alderman Smethurst (chairman), Councillor J. Wilkin- son, Messrs. C.Jackson Holmes, T.Pemberton, SlVright, and T. Locker ; James Armitage Rushworth, licimee of the Horse end Jockey, Winwick-road, was summoned for permitting drunkenness on his premises on the 17th April last. Mr. Steel represented defendant. The Chief Constable said that at twelve o'clock on Sattfralay, the 17th just, Chief Inpector Williamsenand Detective Reddington went to the Horse and Jockey with a warrant for the arrest of John McLay, who had committed an assault in connection with a strike at Whyman's. McLay was in a drunken condition, and on the way to the Police Station behaved in a disgraceful manner. Chief Inspector Williamson said he arrested McLay on the premises and he was in a drunken condition. By Mr. Steel ; There were over 20 people in the house at the time. McLay was one of the men who had teen employed at Whymen'e. He saw Mallao was drunk, but be did not say anything to Mrs. Rushworth at the time, as he was anxious to get the man as quickly as possible. She was a woman of experience so far as the licensing business was concerned, for she had lived in a public house the whole of her life. Detective Reddington and P.C. Holland gave corro- borative evidence. John MoLay, moulder, Appleton-street, said that at about 11.10 on the morning of April 17th he had a pint of beer at the Three Pigeons in Dallam-lane. At about twelve o'clock he went to the club-room behind the Horse and Jockey, but before going there he had several drinks. He left there at about 12.30 and went as far as Tanners'-lane. Be afterwards went to the Horse and Jockey, but was not served with anything. As soon as he got into the Horse and Jockey the police tame in. The Chief Constable said that the witnes had told an entirely different story to that which he told when he gave evidence the previous Tuesday. - In answer to Mr. Steel, witness said he had no drink -in the Horse and Jockey, and had only just sat down when the police came in. Mr. Steel said the charge against Mr. Rushworth was one of permitting drunkenness, and it was for him to satisfy the Bench that he took reasonable steps to prevent drunkenness on his premises. The Chief Constable could not do any other than take the steps be had done. After hearing the evidence of his witnesses be thought the Bench would have no hesita- tion in saying that that was a c,se for inquiry but that, Mr. Rushworth had taken every step to prevent drunken- ness on his premises. He submitted that there had been no neglect and the case ought to be dismissed. George Spiek., moulder, Appleton-street, said that as soon as MeLay sat down the police came in the home, and as for as he knew McLay was not drunk. Mrs. Rushwoith said there were twenty or thirty men in the house when McLay entered. It was the first time he bad been in the house that day, and just after he entered, and whilst she was giving change to another man, the police came in. By the Chief Constable: It was not correct to say that McLay V, as seated. Witness continuing, said she could give no reason why McLay told su entirely different story that day to what he told last week. Mr. S. Wright: What condition was McLay in when be came into your house? Witness: I could see be had had semething to drink. At this point the Bench stopped the case and Alder- ansn Smethurst said the prosecution would be dismissed. The magistrates felt that the police had done their duty in bringing the case forward. The Chief Constable: You really supported Mr. Steel's compliment t! at we were justified. (Laughter.) Alderman Smethurst: Quite so. ALLEGED BiIASS ROBBERY. PRISONERS COMMITTED FOR TRIAL. At the Warrington Police Court, on Tuesday, a youth named Alfred Barrosv,Bond-street, Sankey Bridges, was charged with stealing fifteen brass steps from the iron- w-oike of Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co., Ltd., Liverpool- road, some time between August, 1908, and April, 1909. For receiving the brass knowing it to have been stolen Joseph Matthews, Lythgoes-lane, as also charged. Mr. F. G. Taylor prosecuted, and Mr. A. Browne defended Matthews. The CaS3 for the prosecution was to the effect that on the night of April 23rd, the watchman at the works noticed certain brass steps in their usual pot ions in the iron mill. On the following morning the property was discovered to have been taken away. Information was given to the police, who began to make inquiries. It appeared the prisoner Barrow sold the brass to Matthews. The police had circulated a description of the stolen property to the various metal brokers in the town, and Matthews was ono of the brokers who was supplied with a description. In the meantime from other inquires it came to the knowledge of the police that on the very afternoon of the issue of the description Matthews had delivered to a c rter, in the employ of the Midland Railway Company, three bags containing brass for despatch to Glasgow. This delivery occurred within two hours of the time when the prisoner Matthews had denied to the police that he lad seen any of the brass. Two days later he met, Inspector Williamson and further denied knowledge of the brass. To show that tbosel statements were deliberate untruths he (Hr. Taylot) would reed a voluntary statement given by Matthews, which was to the effect that he had bought from the prisoner Barrow large quantities of brass. Matthews must have known that the brass bad not been honestly obtained. He also must have known diet it was against the law for a metal dealer to purchase qtroatilies of brass under 531 b. Evidence was given by Samuel Mules, John Burtham, Elizabeth Matthews, J. C. Brown, P.C. Woolacott, Detective Reddington and P.C. Leonard. For the defence it was suggested by Mr. Browne that the case should be dealt with there instead of sending it for trial to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions. 'the Clerk ruled that, as Matthews had been previously oenvicted, they had no option but to send the case for Mr. Browneepointed out that Matthews purchased the brass from Barrow at a fair market price. He took every peecautioo, as evidenced by the fact that he cautioned Barrow before he made the purchase. Barron stated that he found the metal in an ashbin at an empty house. The magistrates committed prisoners to the Liver- pool quarter sessions. Matthews was allowed bail, him, elf in £2OO, and two other sureties of £lOO each.
