THE "LIT AND PHIL."

Pianoforte and Dramatic Recital.

At the invitation of Dr. Joseph, the members of the Warrington Literary and Philosophical Society and a number of friends were entertained at a pianoforte and dramatic recital by Mr. T. W. Pearce, of Warrington, and Mr. Ernest M. Thompson, of Manchester, in the Church rooms, Cairo-street, on Tuesday evening. There was a large attendance of leading townspeople, and Dr. and Miss Joseph received the guests in the large schoolroom which had been choicely decorated. for the occasion and looked bright and attractive. Here therecital took place and the high class programme was likened to with keen interest and appreciation. Mr. Pearce is a gifted young pianist of . whom much should be heard in the future. His playing evidenced poetic feeling as well as skillful execution, and the whole of his selections were given from memory. His best effort was Chopin's "Ballade in G minor," the alternating moods of passion and playfulness and the. magnificent, stirring climax being finely realized. Another notable item was Beethoven's ever-popular "Sonata in .0 sharp minor " (The Moonlight), the difficult presto agitato being exceptionally well done. The ear-haunting" Etude in E Major" (Chopin) was given with deep eXpression and was one of the gems of the programme. The other pianoforte items were: "Prelude in C sharp minor"' (Rachmaninoff), "Prelude in A flat" (Chopin)," Les Sylvans " (Chaminade), " Etude Mignon " (Schutt)," Grillen " (Schumann), and "Gavotte Imperiale in A" (Janotha). . Mr. Ernest M. Thompson has previously appeared before the. Literary and Philosophical-Society with conspicuous success and on Tuesday he enhanced his reputation. The items were nicely varied and in

every -case he appeared to present the true conception of the author's meaning, pathos and humour, lofty declamation and gentle.raillery all having 'their part. Particularly pleasing was "The Wooing of Katherine" from Shakespeare's "Henry V "the part of Katherine being presented with delightful naivete by Miss Dorothy Browne, while Mr. Thompson portrayed the bluff yet ardent lover to the. life. Mr. Thompson's contributions, all of which afforded great pleasure, were" Sam's Valentine" fro ni "Pickwick Papers" (DiekenB), " The two Francs " (a character study) (F. Poole)," Doo'ey on Modern Educational Methods" (F. P. Dann), Ankin on Decimal Coinage" (B. Pain) and " Hints to Orators."

During an interval refreshments were served in the upper schoolroom. The thanks of the assembly to Dr. Joseph were cordially expressed on the proposition of Mr. A. J. Jolley seconded by Mr. J. Rymer Young

Salvation Army.

Colonel Edward Wright, of the London Headquarters of the Salvation Army gave an interesting lecture on Sunday afternoon in the Academy-street Barracks entitled "A recent run through Mexico." The Colonel graphically told of his arrival in the bay and bow he was struck cith the natural beauty of the country. He mentioned that he had great difficulty in getting an interview v%ith President Dias in relation to the

foundation of a branch of the Salvation Army there. However, he managed to secure a promise from him that he would do his best towards the furtherance of the movement. The Colonel told of his endeavour to attend diffierent places of worship on the Sunday there, and find kindred spirits. At last he received a fine welcome from a Mexic in boy whom he hoped some day would be of the pioneers in the work of the Army in Mexico. Concluding the Colonel exhorted the large audience to help all they possibly could to send helpers out to found a branch of the Army in Mexico, where the President had promised to give them a hearty welcome.

Wycliffe Sunday School Anniversary. The Wycliffe Sunday school anniversary was held last Sunday. In the morning the Rev. F. Y. Leggatt. M.A. (Manchester) gave an instructive address to tile children. Afterwards he based his s(trmon on the Garden of Eden and the Garden of Gethsemane. The former, he said, represented the joys and pleasures of youth, and the latter the age of responsibility.—The anthem "Be glad and rejoice" was sung, Mr. P. 0. Bailey being the soloist

In the afternoon there was a service of song, the hymns being rendered in an admirable manner, evidencing a good amount of training on the part of Mr. W. Brown.. Mr. William Beswick presided, apd Mr. J. Upson, J.P., was the speater. Miss J. Armstrong and Mr. F. Brown rendered solos.

Mr. J. UPON observed that he had e very diffloult task before him that afternoon for he had to interest both young and old. He based his remarks on Jesus and true greatness. In these days of national suspicions, of panic fears, widespread clamouring for Dreadnoughts when nation frantically strove against nation, when Dreadnoughts, piled up munitions of warfare, when arm'es and navies were daily growing, it might be thought bold and quite extravagant to tie compared to the claims of the meek and lowly Jesus, as King of the world, and yet He was King,—not in the sense of course in which the disciples of all expacted Him to be King. He was King, not in the extent of His territories, not in His abundant wealth, not in the might and power of His government. He was King of the greatest of Kings because he was the most dominant force; because He was the mightiest inspiration; because He was the most dynamic power in the world to day. Kingship and greatness in the ordinary sense of the word implied vast power, great wealth, wide territory, and above all things, the exercise of authority. The Lord Jesus Christ brought • a now idea into the world. Christ said, "Whosoever will be great amongst you shall be your miuister and whosoever shall be chief shall be servant of all." That is to say that Jesus Cbrist taught a new idea altogether about t rue greatness.hie showed that true greatness was onlyt o be achieved through service, which He delibrately chose for Himself and which He called upon all airs to follow. The world's greatness could only be obtained as a rule by cost to others. Take the suacessful soldier; we praised him according to the proportion of victories be bars won. His victories had been won by blazing home- steads, by the shedding of blood, by the expenditure of millions of treasure. The mighty conquerors whoso names occupied the roll of history of this and other lands, had been men who had trampled under foot whole bodies of. people. Take to-day the multi-rnilliaualre. Frequently his giiatness had been won in speculation. He had enriched himself as othersdrad been made poorer. Take the man to-day who was revelling in-millions, because he had success- fully cornered wheat, at the cost of infinite sufferina- of men, women and children throughout the whore world. The grortnrss which Christ wanted us to obtain could only be by cost to ourselves redeemed by .the precious blood-of Christ. Earthly greatness called forth powers and talents which they possibly did not possess, but the greatness which Christ wanted aas the areatness we all might achieve. Christ's g•eitness was high character, and high ch tractor meant high position in Christ's kingdom. Ccintinuing .he maintained that Jesus was King in spite of our infidelity, disloyalty and forgetfulnes§, and Jesus ruled in the minds of men. If they got Christ into their lives Ho would become not only the dominating influence of their hearts, but He would become the dominating influence of our international policy. When His principles extended in all the councils of the nations then would peace reign. (Applause.) showed a photograph of 'a group of the natives deaply abs Orbed in the pastime. Mohammedanism prohibited the use of alcoholic drinks and the result was great quietnesks and orderliness in the streets and cafes. The Arabs adopted an-attitude of indifference with regard to the education of the girls, but it was gratifying that some of the leading French ladies living in Algiers were realizing their responsibilities in this matter and were teaching the girls arts and crafts. From Algiers, the lecturer proceeded to the famous Sahara desert, and one of the most interesting places visited was Tirogad, a perfect specimen of a Roman provincial town which was dug out of the sand about twinty-five years ago, just. as the remains of Pompeii had been unearthed. Many of the buildings still standing were evidence of the fine civic patriotism which existed amongst the Romans. The value of. sanitation and cleanliness were fully recignized and there were extensive public baths. There was a theatre to accommodate 4,0(0 people and other relics

were fine statues and mosaics. All these things showed the immense importance which the Romans attached to a well-organized town s's a means to a happy social existence, and as a centre of civilizing influence. Many interesting inscriptions were found on the sails of the houses. On one of them a gay young man, who must have lived many ages ago and rever expected that the inscription would be read in these cla3s had recorded his view of human life which was "to hunt, to bathe, to laugh, to play, that is the meaning of human life." It made one feel how similar human nature was in its follies and triumphs over all the span of centuries. Other places iteluded in the lecturer's itinerary were niskra,.,a famous pleasure resort, Constantine a town built high upon a rock and said to have withstood thirty or forty sieges, Tunis, Carthage, at one time one of the most important cities in the world but now in ruins, Malta, Syracuse, Palermo, and the neighbourhood Of Messina. The lecture concluded with a set of views showing the terrible devastation wrour2ht by the earthquake at Niessin•P, and a number of British blue jackets engaged in tiro work of succouring the injured. On the motion of Mr. F. W. MONKS, seconded by Mr." J. RymErt YOUNG, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Drummond, to Mr. Hill for the loan of the lantern and to Mx. Jones for manipulating it. Refreshments were afterwards served.

Our great Shakespeare had made it familiar to us in "Antony and Cleopatrav," and we also thought of the great periods of Grecian and Roman history. Coming to the present time, it was on the borders of ti a Mediterranean that the great battle took place t.etween the young Turk and the old Turk, so, that in the subject of that lecture they -were in touch with the past and the present. The Rev. W. H. llnummosn, after recalling the many friendships he formed at Cairo-street, entereda vigorous protest against what he desctibed as an act of" sheer, absolute prafanation and vandalism "—the erection of the hideous wall behind their beautiful new schools. There was only one person in Warrington towards whom he bad *my unkind feeling, and that was the person mho built that wall. Proceeding with his lecture, he gave a highly entertaining and instructive account of his journeyings through the sunny lands bordering on the Mediterranean and pictured in such glowing - phrases the many notable things he had seen, that one longed to have the opportunity of a like experience. He started on his holiday in December 1907 and was away until the end of March 1908. The flmt place described was Algiers—once the centre of a wild piraticAl life, and now divided into two portions.

He took for his text "Never imtn so spake "(Sip. John, 7-46) and said that the Romans had been instructed to take Him. They were waiting fora convenient moment to take Him, without exciting the people. They returned without Him, and when asked why,t hey replied, "Never man so snake." The rev, gentleman slid that Shakes- peare and Milton possessed a great influence, but there was a limit even to their powers. Christ was a man who dropped his spiritual sayings as a millionaire his penny. Christ's speech was conspicuously poetic and simple. Some had spoken more • eloquently, but they had nothing like the power. He had no bursts of eloquence, no flights of imagination; he was nsver really caught up and carried away with His speech ; He was marvellously self-possessed; He spoke in proverbs, sayings and parables; He was calm and knew the spiritual world of which be spoke; ,He was in the bosom of the Father ; He was perfectly at home in that invisible laud—the eternal land. Christ's speech might be likened to the flow of the Nile.: It was calm majestic and carried all before it without strain or noise. His speech came not as bursts of , foam, as came tbe speech of the prophets, it came calmly, quietly, solemnly, as a great light, penetrating illuminating the darkness. As a ray of light His speech entered tho minds of men. In the speech of the Scribes there was a certain aloofness. It was hsrd and ;mechanical. It was aloof from the people. Christ's speech was just the opposite. It was gracious and sympathetic. There was no touch of uncharitableness- it was gentle and tender. His speech came not as a lightning flash; but as the light of dawn, after a night of pain. Great men generally did not advertise them- selves ; bat it was the very opposite with Jesus. Ho drew waention to himself; He testified to Himself ; He elevated Himself; He raid: "Believe, in Me, learn of Me, come receive Me." and " I am the light cf the world." WARRINGTON ADULT SCHOOL.

A Progressive Organization.

The Warrington Adult School, which seeks to serve the purpose of a rtwl brotherhood, is making gratifying progress and it has just found a new awl more commodi- ous home in Academy-place. The opining of the schoolroom was celebrated on Saturday evening when a well-attended meeting was bald under the presidency of .the Mayor (Alderman Forshaw). Supporting the Mayor on the platform were: Messrit .James Dodds, Arthur Mounfleld, J. A. Ransom() (president of the Warrington Adult School), Edwin Gilbert (secretary of the National, Adult School Council), Gilbert Forrest (Manchester), and Miss Dale (Pendleton). The SECRETARY (Mr. Bradley), said letters bed been received from the Revs. Canon Morley Stevensen, C. Harvey Cook; NV. Bracecamp, A. J. Bratt, Mr. C. E. Parker and Mr. Bradshaw. r The CHAIRMAN said that as the Mayor of Warrington he was very pleased to be there that night'; . but he could not say very much with regard to the Adult School, because be did not know anything about, it until a few days ago, when he was asked to preside at that meeting, and a pamphlet was handed to him. He certainly found much in it that was ckf a worthy kind. He understood it was for the uplifting of men and women, the improvement of the mind, body and soul. lie was sure that with objects of that kind, it certainly deserved the support of every true Christian, and of every good citizen. He should himself be very pleased to be a member of their Society,—(applause)—if they would allow him to do so, because he understood it was entirely unsectarian and therefore all creeds could join it. He wished the Society every possible success. He understood that that meeting was really for the opening of the new premises. Concluding, he said : Tis religion that can give, sweetest pleasures while we live ; Tis religion can supply solid comforts when we die. (Applause.) Miss DALE. of Pendleton Adult Scheol, emphasised the fact that they were not bringing women away from their work and thereby neglecting it, when they came to the Adult School. When they went back to their work at home, they went back with better ideals, and better prepared for their work in every way. A woman's place was certainly in the home, but they were trying to stiow that if they could got the women from the home for an hour or so during the week they would be better for it. The Adult School extended the hand of friendship to women, and gave them a wider Outlook upon things.

Mr. JAMES DODDS observed that he did not know much about the movement, but from what he did know he wished God-speed to it. There was very much room for the work, especially as regarded women. The schools would fill a gap for women. H 3 could quite realise that helping people as they did had more influence than the pre4ching in the pulpit. They studied the Bible, and there could be no better study. Look at the power it had in the world, how it suited every class of men in every position of life. He liked the idea that they talked among thernselves, rather than one man talkine all the time. (Applause). In con- clusion he said he was very pleased to hear that the Mayor had joined the society. (Hear, hear). He would also become a member. (Applause). - Mr. EDWIN GILI3ERT .naintained that if the school was going to be permanent it would have to grow slowly. They wanted to get men one by one so that when they came into the school they would find out pretty much what the school' was, and that it had demands upon each of them. The Society in Warring- ton was only 15 months old, and they lied made good p-ogress. Last year was the first year that there had been so many women's schools opened as men's. This year the men had opened nearly 80 schools, and the women's schools were almest as numerous. The Adult Schools had a total membership of 110,000. There were already 1,640 schools in England. People had wondered how it, was that it change Lad been made in the lives of men, and were quite surprised when the Adult School was mentioned. The Adult School movement worked quietly and gradually. Two hundred and twenty new schools were onered fast year, and this was so rapid that one could hardly say to what dimenvionl the movement would reach. There was a spirit of absolute democracy. There was a spirit of real fellowsbip and brotherhoed which they felt as they came into their meet- ings. They wanted to get into seciety a spirit of brother- hood, and they wanted it in capital and labour, and then there would not be much difficulty in solving our social questions. The message of the Adult Schorr) was that there were marvellous capacities in each of our lives, and the great spirit of God was dwel!ing -in us. Con- cluding, he said there ought tO be at least twelve Adult Schools in Warrington. (Applause.) Mr. J. A. RANSOME at this point said the room had been decorated by the members, and he was very pleased with it. They wanted to start a library and obtain a piano. Mr. GILBERT FORREST congratulated the Society on having such a good room in which to conduct their school. There was not an institution anywhere in which there were so many ways of doing good. They were gcing to turn out better men who were going to be better citizens than - Warrington had ever known before, and their women would make their homes brighter and purer. (Applause). It wits encouragement to them in Manchester and Stockport, to see how much IVarrington was going ahead in adult school work. The central feature of the adult school movement was Bible study. They appealed to reople on the social and intellectual sides. Every man had a right to take part in the discussion and also in the service. Mr. Arthur MOUNFIELD said he bad been in close touch with the Adult School movement for 13 years. He thought the atmosphere that had been engendered in the Adult School was precisely the atmosphere in the early church. The early church were untrammelled, they were seeking after truth, and that was the`position of the Adult School. People iv. nt to the Adult School to get inspiraion and to get light to enable them to help and influence somebody else. The great battles of the future were being settled in their Adult Schools. It would not be in the Hous6 of Commons. They might be cow:unmated there, but they were going to be settled in inch institutions as theirs. There was a vast difference between tha Kingdom of God and the Church. The Church miaht be the course for realising the Kingdom, but the Church was not the Kingdom. The Kingdom was va‘tly broader and greater thin the Church and the Adult School belonged rather to the Kingdom than that of the Church. Concluding he said that in the Adult School they had something which was going to be the bringing about of the K;ngcloar God. (Applause).

FERRO-CONCRETE SCHEME DECIDED UPON. The following recommendations of the Bridges Sub- committee were submitted by the General Purposes Comm.ttee for approval : " That the row bridge to be erected at Bridge-foot be constructed of ferro-concrete. _ "That the Council be recommended to obtain tenders for a bridge 7u feet and 80 feet wide respectively, instead of 60 feet and 80 feet respectively. "That the lines of the proposed new bridge be in continuation of the lines of Bridge-street. "That training walls be constructed at each end of the bridge, as shown on the plan submitted. "That a consulting engineer be appointed. "That the plan prepared by the Borough Surveyor and now submitted by him, showing the line of the proposed new bridge, be approved." COft. would be wide enough, but be would bow to the wish of the manufacturers.

The Chamber of Commerce, which represented nearly all the principal manufacturers, had passed a resolution more than Clleft in favour of a bridge 80 feet wide, and also either tho • Ratepayers' or Tradesmen's Association—he did not know which—and whenever an 80ft. bridge had been , suggested it had been received with enthusiasm. ilet had advocated that the four main streets should be SR feet wide, and people were now regretting that, Bridge- street had been made so narrow. With reL:ard to Blackfriars Bridge, it was a disgrace to Manchester. Councillor ROGERS said he hal been approached by many people and their advice was "If you are going to build a bridge build a proper one. Don't spoil the ship , for a pennyworth of tar." Councillor BARTON said a 60 feet or a 70 feet bridge; would be ample for a grebt number' of years. It would lint be long before they would want another bridge— perhaps two—and they would not be at a great distance from each other, and a great deal of the Cheshire tralßa would then be tapped. _ _ _ _

Councillor TOOLE raid there was not a unanimous feeling that the bridge khould be 80 feet wide. Whets they considered that if the bridge were 80 feet wide it would be 4.feet wider than the widest part of Winwick- road, near Guest's tannery, it appeared that such scheme would be needless expense. In the course of further discussion, Councillor VtittaptsoN said it had not been decided that they wera to have a bridge 80 feet wide. The difference betweea a 60ft. bridge and a 70ft. bridge would be about £2,100, and that was no big thing, and it all depended upon the number of years they got in which to pay back the money. A bridge 60 feet wide would be sufficient for the present, but not for the future. Alder.nan SMETHURST, in reply, said they were not

A FERRO-CONCRETE STRUCTURE.

Alderman SNIETHUEIST then formally moved :—" That the new 'midge to be erected at Bridge-foot be con- structed of ferro-concrete. _ _

This was seconded by Alderman BOLTON and carried without discussion.

COIMCWOr ROGERS : I object. It ought to be an iron bridge. , DISCUSSION ON THE WIDTH. _ _

Alderman SMETHURST then moved :—" That the Council be recommended to obtain tenders for a bridge 7ort. end SOft. wide respectively, instead of Oft. and 80ft. respectively. Councillor PEMBERL.N characterised 70 or 80ft. as a ridiculous width. _ _

The Malron : That is your opinion. Councitlor PEmmiaToN : It is not a question of opinion. We have important evidence in this country in towns very much larger then Warrington as to the width of bridges. Blackfriars Bridge in Manchester is only 40ft. wide and the population of the district is very great; in fact, more traffic goes over it in one day than over Warrington Bridge in a month. Alderman SMETHURST : Blackfriara Bridge is being altered. Councillor PEMBERTON instanced Waterloo Bridge in London which was along 45ft. wide, and said more traffic went over it in twenty-four hours than passed over Warrington Bridge in twenty-feur days. If the new bridge was constructed 80ft. wide it would be like Palmyra-square. (Laughter.) He contended that a 50ft bridge would be sufficient. Go to 60ft. if they liked, and then they would have not only plenty of room, but room to wander about. (Laughter.) Every thousand of expenditure they put on the bridge they put a debt on the tonn for 50 year. All those improvements should be curied out in the most busi- nesslike and economical way. Why should they build a bridge that would last 200 years? A 60ft. bridge wou'd give four moving lines of traflll—two one way and two another—and it would serve for quite 100 years. The present bridge was only 27ft. 8:n. wide and yet there was on stoppage of traffic. They cauld btend on the bridge end not see more than a dozen carts on it. It must also be borne in mind that additional new bridges were contemplated and they would relieve the traffic over Warrirign Bridge. While they ought to have whit was adequate for their needs, they had no right to say they would have what was as g -ied and as big as any other towns had. if they could save £3,000 or £4,000 on the scheme._ they could do very well with the money. There was the widening of Buttermarket-street, the reconstruction of the sewers, and other heavy expenditure before them. He moved that the recommendation be not confirmed. Councillor CUNNINOTON seconded. Councillor HAwTtiouN said they would make a mis- take if they were too economical in iegard to the width of the bridge, and he hoped if they erred at all they would err in having the bridge too wide rather than too narrow. They ought to take into consideration not only the natural increase of traffic consequent upon the growth of the population, but an abormal increase which would have to be dealt with. The traffic was small now compared with what it, would be in a few years, in consequence of the growth of mechanical propulsion of vehicles. He hoped the new bridge would be constructed on artistic lines. _ _

_ The MAYOR said he had good -reason to believe that if the bridge was made 80ft. wide they would get con- siderable financiAl assistance from the large manu- facturers. _

Councillor PEmBERTON: Name. The MAYou : lam not going to name them to-night. (Hear, hear.) Alderman BURTON endorsed what the Mayor had said. It was the wish of some of the manufacturers that the bridge should be 80ft. wide. Ire thought himself that

WELL-KNOWN JOCKEY RETIRES.

HERBERT MORNINGTON CANNON. M. Cannon, the celebrated jockey, has definitely decided to retire from the saddle. He is probably the most successful rider of his day ; altogether he has won over 2,000 races, and for many years he shared the chief riding honours with Tom Loates. Betwem 1891 and 1859 he steered wel.l over a hundred winners every year, his most successful year being 1895, when he won 184 races. In 1897 he won the Two Thousand, Derby, and St. Leger on Flying Fox,

WARRINGTON CHAPTERS. MORE ABOUT THE TEMPERANCE MOVEMENT AND 1.73 ORIGIN.

.esI'VA.I%,-

Several enquiries have come to us from friends and correspondents upon the subject of our " Warrington Chapter" of last week. Many find it difficult to understand how so much confusion can have arisen in a matter of history which is so comparatively recent. We are reminded that the story of the 'Seven men of Preston " has been univers- ally accepted and its accuracy scarcely called into question until after a lapse of seventy years.

To those who know the facts it is not a matter of surprise that Preston should have

gained the credit of originating the Temper- anCe Movement. The true pioneers were Quakers and had a Quakerly disregard for the praise of men. They spent no time in self-advertisement and used no needless rhetoric. At Preston a halo of romance was thrown around the advocates and the story of the so-called " Seven Men," touched the popular imagination.

Mr. Livesey excepted, though three were con- sistent and useful advocates. „

This surely disposes for ever of the " Seven Men" story and it is incredible that the people of Preston should attempt to keep it alive. Year by year, however, as the first of September comes round the Press is plied with paragraphs concerning the "famous anniversary," and the offer of the loan of an illustration of the pledge the "Seven Men" signed. This much-prized relic, the Preston Pledge has had much more attention than it has merited. As everyone knows all the signatures are in the handwriting,of Joseph Livesey, and he, on his own confession, did not write it until years after it was said to have been signed. It was written to pre- serve a memorandum of the pledge and those who were at the meeting. The wording of the pledge is as follows: "We agree to abstain from all liquors of an intoxicating quality, whether ale, porter, wine or ardent spirits, except as medicine." This is a simple pledge of a purely personal character, with- out any promise to advocate the cause,or dis-

countenance the use of liquors amongst others. In its omission it is in striking con- trast with the Warrington pledge of 1830. If we should assume that the Preston story is quite true it would in no way affect our claim for Warrington for the date of the Society here was two years and five months before the date of the Preston pledge. To complete the proof in favour of Warring- ton nothing remains save that we should be able to prove what has seemed so probable to many, that Livesey and the Preston men got their first information concerning total abstinence from this district. This proof , appears in an article written by Livesey himself in a magazine of which he was editor.

THE "SEVEN MEN OF PRESTON "FABLE.

It is time however that we recognized that the story of the Seven Men of Freston is a piece of imagination. We can appeal to no better an authority than Dr. Dawson Barns. He has had no rival theory to support and has been able to look at things in what Bacon called a " dry light." He is a most competent Temperance historian, and as he wrote before any claim was made on behalf of Warrington, he discussed the story purely upon its merits. In his "Temperance in the Victorian Age" Dr. Burns wrote "None of the seven except Mr. Livesey, and perhaps Mr. John Brodbelt were of any-temperance note, nor did they form a society or even a committee for the promotion of Total Abstinence." Five years later he wrote a vigorous

article which appeared in the " AlliancetMovement spread' rapidly. This is fact; all the rest of the every sound historian of Temperance has been obliged to condemn. traditional story is pure romance. The ' seven

THE MANCHESTER SOCIETIES.

After establishing the Society at Stockton Heath George Harrison Birkett travelled to Manchester. His friend William Wood of that city had helped him here and he returned the comradeship by advocating total abstinence in Manchester. They were able to gain ready hearers among the Society of Friends and a Total Abstinence Society was quickly formed in the Mount-street meeting house. The date on which the Roll Book was signed was May 12th, 1830. The

THE PRESTON PLEDGE. The unlikliness of Preston having formed the pioneer society is manifest upon a study .of the Temperance pledge which was adopted there. The Society of 1832, about which so much has been said, was not a Total Abstinence Society at all. Its pledge read : " We do voluntarily agree that we will abstain from the use of ardent spirits our- selves and will not give nor offer them to othets except as medicines. And if we use any other liquors it shall at all times be with great moderation, and we will to the utmost of our power discountenance all the causes and practices of intemperance." Such a pledge allowed wine and beer and left those who signed it pretty much where it found them. This was confessed by John King, Livesey's best helper, and one of the "Seven." The members of the society, he said, " get drunk on beer. Preston folks do not get drunk on spirits. We shall do no good until we get our members to do without beer and all other intoxicating drink." And King set an example by signing a private pledge in August, 1832. But the Preston Society was not so minded, ansl the fact that it allowed its members to

drink beer and wine showed how far it lagged behind the Warrington Society of Total Abstinence. The date on which a society was first formed in Preston on an Abstinence pledge was April 18th, 1834. This was not the ordinary society but a branch society for youths. But Preston seemed to think that total abstinence was a risky thing even for children for the pledge was a promise to abstain "for one year" only. The following year, 1835,brought better light,and on March 25th the Preston Society adopted a total abstinence pledge. It was just five years behind Warrington, but by its zeal won for itself a place of honour in the annals of Temperance. To many this recital' of history will have little interest; but to those who are alive to the magnitude of the Temperance cause it will be anything but unimportant. What- ever honours attaches to the starting of one of the world's greatest movements belongs to Warrington. To quote Mr. P. T. Winskill whose "History of the Temperance Move- ment" is the standard work on the subject : "The first bona-fide Total Abstinence Society in England is now conclusively proved to have been that organized and worked with success in Providence Chapel, Stockton Heath, near Warrington, and known as the yv-arrington Temperance Society." The modest, earnest men could hardly have realized as they leaned‘over the pulpit in the little chapel that they were speaking to such purpose. That they knew the gravity of their cause is evident from what they wrote and spoke. It can hardly be said of George Harrison Birkett that he "builded better than he knew," for his manifesto contemplates a movement and sketches a programme as wide as civilization, but neither he nor his friends could have foreseen

the great outcome of his efforts. After nearly eighty years of progress we can see the work in better propprtion and estimate its worth. We do well therefore to honour

the memory and courage of George Harrison Birkett, and rejoice that he found his first disciples in our midst. • [The cartoons with which this article is illustrated were issued locally in the early thirties of the last century. They are the work of an unknown artist.] A.M.

MY LIFE STORY.

I was born at Hall Green, near Mow Cop, on the borders of Staffordshire and Cheshire, on March 18th, 1836. My father, James Skelland, was a shoemaker, but when I was very young he obtained employment at the Hanley blast furnaces, and we removed to that town. Times were very. bad then, and workmen used to go through the Potteries in mobs, demanding work and food. Bread was hard to get, the price being as high as ninepence per 41b. loaf—and not very good at that. The next change in my experi- ence came when we left Hanley, and went to live at a country place about six miles distant from there. I remember my grand- father, who was a farmer, removed our belongings in one of his waggons. The only house available in those parts stood alone in the middle of a field and was called the White Hall. It had the reputation of being haunted and one night when we were waiting for father to return home, we were rather alarmed to hear uncanny sounds. The door opened mysteriously but instead of a ghost, there walked in a harmless cat! My father had got work in a stone pit, and had to work 12 hours per day for 2s. He mended and made boots during his spare time at night, and he used to take some of the boots to a beerhouse to• be raffled for. He had monthly reckonings. I should not like those times of corn taxa- tion and dear food to return again. We were a family of five and our living was scanty and plain. My mother was a clean, indus- trious woman and made the best of every- thing. ' She used to wash and mend my clothes on Saturday night and send me to Sunday school the next morning. Of " schooling" in the accepted sense, I had none, because I went to work at the age of geven. I attended a class conducted by an engine driver at the Wesleyan school, but I owed my education almost entirely to Sunday schools. It is impossible adequately to express my gratitude to those earnest men and women who give their time .to instructing the young in those schools. My earliest occupation was to go " gleaning " corn, which my mother used to send to the mill to be ground into flour. Then I worked for a time with the reapers— that was the first employment for which I received wages—and later, during the con- struction of the railroad from Congleton to Harecastle and Kidsgrove, I helped in carry- ing bricks from the maker. At the age of ten I obtained a situation at the iron works at Kidsgrove, having to walk two miles morning and evening. I was very attentive to my work, always trying to please my employers and to help the other workmen, and I remained at Kidsgrove for a con- siderable time. However, my brother came to work at Dallam Forge, Warrington, and when I happened to come to the town on a visit, the forge manager wanted me to enter the employ of the firm as well. I remember the advice given to me-by a woman who used to sell herb beer near the works: "My lad, if thou hast a good home,don't come here; they call it the traveller's rest." I suppose she was alluding to the constant changes that were taking place, men coming and going every week. Warrington did not attract me then, and I returned to Kidsgrove, but two years later—in July 1856—1 changed my mind and took up the position of a roller at Dallam Forge. Looking back over• the fifty odd years I have spent at Warrington, I have never had reason to regret coming here. In those days the forge belonged to Messrs. Neil and Com- pany, and the chief engineer was Mr. E. Beck, who lived in a house adjoining the works.

He was, therefore, able to hear when anything went wrong with the engines, and would often come into the works in the middle of the night to attend to breakdowns. The concern has made gigantic strides since then, for not only have additional mills been built at Dallam but the forge at Bewsey has been opened,and many improvements in machinery etc. have also been affected. When I came to Warrington there were fields where Bewsey Forge now stands and also in the neighbour- hood which has since developed into Bewsey- street. The whole town has undergone great changes, which the Writers of previous "life stories" have touched upon. I worked at Dallam continuously about 37 years, with the exception of two years during which I was suffering from the effects of a severe accident sustained whilst following my employment. I left Dallam at the time of the great coal strike having, on the whole, worked very agreeably with masters and men.

Your readers will probably wish to know something of the history of the institution of which I was the founder and with which I have continued to be closely identified—the Warrington Ragged School Mission. Long before the foundation of the Mission in 1887, the sad lot of the ragged children for whom nobody seemed to care,had touched my heart and made me feel that something must be done for their benefit. I was in those days superintendent of a Sunday School at the Dallam-lane Chapel, but in order to have greater freedom I resigned that position and, becoming a visitor, went out into some of the alleys and courts of Warrington and persuaded a num- ber of children to make their first acquaint- ance with a Sunday School. In three or four Sundays the children thus brought under Christian influence numbered over sixty, but some of the officials looked with displeasure on the introduction of these ragged, unkempt little ones into the school, and threatened that they would not let the scholars who usually attended sit by them. For a time, therefore, I had to relinquish the work but determined to start a Ragged School whenever opportunity presented. I was able to acquire the old school in Dallam- lane on the removal of the congregation to Buckley-street, but the conditions were far from promising, for there was not a scrap of furniture and no funds with which to buy any. I talked of my difficulties to those at home and at last, a daughter of mine offered to give me the contents of her money box-3s. 6d. I believe it was—as a start. I was so encouraged by this act of practical sympathy that I deter- mined to commence the school and trust to the generosity of friends and sympathers for financial support. In the provision of seating accommodation I was liberally helped by three friends—Mr. R. A. Naylor, Mr. Smethurst and Mr. Joseph Mounfield. On the first Sunday in January 1887, the first Ragged Sunday School in Warrington was opened. I was assisted by Mr. Robert Dale and my daughter, and in the afternoon,when the other schools had opened, we went Out towards Town's End to gather together some of the poorest boys and girls_ About one hundred responded to our invitation to come to the ragged school, and a pathetic sight they presented, for many were com- pletely destitute of the comforts of life. They crouched round the stove and some fell asleep under the influence of the heat. We sang a hymn and talked kindly to them. The school has been undenominational from the first and I hope it will remain so. In a circular issued at the time there occurred the following sentence : "This, school is intended for the poor, ragged and destitute children of the town and is open every , Sunday afternoon at 2.30. All children of this class,who do not attend anywhere, are affec- tionately invited to the school,where they will meet with kind friends and a hearty welcome." I am thankful to say that the school's work has been attended with considerable success. Many of the boys and girls who have passed through it are now in situations and doing well, and a few of the oldest scholars have become active workers in the school. Were it not for the money raised by the drum and fife band the school would long since have had to close its doors, for these boys have been the means of collecting over £6OO towards the building fund as well as-large sums annually for the general income. Mr. Owen,of whose devoted labours I cannot speak too highly, and myself have travelled over 20,000 miles on the railway and over 4,000 miles on foot with the band which, in addi- tion to being the main support of the school has been a great help to the lads in providing food and clothing. When the old County Court in Bank-street was no longer required by the Government, the premises were secured by the late Mr. Robert Garnett, one of the vice-presidents of our school, for £BB5. It was decided to alter the building for the purposes of the school and the total cost was ,E 1,450 all of which has been paid off. The latest development of our work is the opening of a labour home which has already been of some assistance to a large number of unemployed and destitute men who have passed through the town. One of the most gratifying incidents in my life ' took place in 1904, when the teachers and workers of the mission presented me with an illuminated address and photographic groups of the teachers and children. The address stated: "Many a young man and woman at the present day rejoice for the teaching and guidance you have given them in their early years and thank God that they were lifted from their miserable home con- ditions by the influence they received at the Ragged School." The thought that I have been of some service to the poorest of the poor always cheers and encourages me, and I hope the Ragged School will long con- tinue to justify its existence by lending a helping hand to those most in need.

. . • . * • • . .

COUNCILLOR JAMES SKELLAND.

lINSLAKED LIME.

A danger that threatens all who are engaged on new building operations—which is indeed a rather constant condition of landowners with a property of any con- siderable extent—is iedicated by the account of three half-finished houses lately destroyed by fire started by a heavy shower falling on unslaked lime. It is, of course a risk which is pretty obvious; but it is so contrary to the ordinary idea that a big fall of water, far from quenching a fire, should be actually its occasion, that it is not appreciated as it ought to be. The man who is a builder or mason by trade ought to be fully aware of the danger of leaving lime in its unslaked state near any inflammable materials, but the ordinary house- carpenter or gardener, surning his hand to a bit of building work on occasion, is likely indeed not to pnliee it.—" Country Life."

Well, he took me by the arm and began to walk me up and down the narrow path. "They're not tipsy," nays he, "they're just glad, Paddy. It's a long story, my boy, and a good one. But I'll have to tell it you in two minutes."

"Ay," says I, "and it's the story of the child, no doubt." He nodded his head. He's a fine handsome lad, with a wicked wisp of brown curls over his handsome forehead, and two clear blue eyes which should go deep into any woman's heart. And he never looked handsomer than he did this night. "Her story, of course, Paddy. I have wen her by a trick, my boy. Don't say now that I was wrong to go to my cousin's house, for it was little Martha who put the first notion of it into my head." "Did the parson call you out?" "No, he called me in lest the neighbout-s should see." . _

"Did he complain of his ship coming horns --the poor devil of a turn?" "II was a little awkward, certainly—but it saved me, Paddy. I just ran over to Cromer to see Mimi, and then came on to London to fit up this house. What will appeal to her, said I, will be a new Maison du bon Tabae." "You've plenty of it here. "1:would take a telescope to see across the room." He was a little crows with me for interrupt- ing him, and, in faith, I was as curious to hcar his story as he to tell it. So I just held my tongue and let him run on freely. "I determined if I could," he said, "to find Mimi a little house in London, which would speak to her of the old days in Paris and lead her to forget that she is among strangers in a strange country. SO I titt,d up this place. You see what kind of a place it is, Paddy—just a replica of the old villa on the Butte, with the very furniture that we used to laugh at there. Then I sent for my friends, and the good fellows came at once. How could they keep away?" "You paid their fares, Harry?" "Yes, and old Georges had an accident with his at the Cabaret of the Tete Noire—so I had to send it twice. But they came, Paddy, and we began to live the old life just as we lived it at Montmartre—and then we wrote to Mimi; we all Wrote to her, and we sat down and waited for her. My God! if you had known what those days of waiting meant to me!' _ _ _

He was deeply-moved, and my heart went cut to, him. I have spoken to you before of his great love for this child—and, to be sure, it is an honest man's devotion, full of fine, chivalrous thoughts .and, so utterly unselfish that it must bring him to abject poverty by and bye. This however, was not the time to speak of it. "But she came .to you, Harry?" said I; "she came to you, man?" , "God be thanked, she did, Paddy. It was last night—these fellows had all gone off to dine in Soho at 'a French cafe no Christian man has ever heard of. I was alone in the house—all my spirit had gone, for Mimi's let- ter seemed to say that she would not come. All the prophets of evil whispered in my cars and promiced me misfortunes while I waited. I lived half a lifetime of poverty, distress, and disappointment—alone in the dark- of the garden looking down upon the lights of London, and asking if they hid Mimi from my sight. You know what moods like these can be—how we seem robbed of every shred of hope, how we say that good fortune will never visit us again--wish almost that our lives were lived. That was my case for two long hours—oh, my dear Paddy, may I never live such hours again." "And then," says I, "then, my dear Harry, you were lilted up to heaven in a jiffy. Elijah didn't beat you at the firing." He laughed like a boy at this, he squeezed my arm as though he would press all the human kindness out of me and add it to his own store. Trust a man in love to be a miser with his sympathies. "As- true as gold, Paddy," says he, "she came at nine o'clock, just when I had put pistols in the balance with laudanum, anti was watching the scale. I can hear the wheels rolling on the gravel now—ah, the roll of the wheels that carry your mistress to you, is there any meeter music in life? " "Did she come alone, Harry?" "The man they call Jack Bendall brought her. I gave him a ten-pound note for him- self and a fiver each for the others of the company. Of course, I didn't guess at firs, that Mimi was in the cab, and my heart started to beat like a fire-pump. She was in, I said, gone back to France perhaps—or even dead. Then, Paddy, I heard her voice! Think of that, old boy, I heard her voice!" "'Twas what was said in the Dublin Courts last week, when Mary Wentworth went for a divorce from old Mike. She heard a voice in the parlour—a female voice----"

"Oh, be serious, Paddy, be serious." "The very words the Judge used. Do you mean to marry her .now you've got her here, Harry?" "Am I a rogue, Paddy? I'd have married her this morning if the priest would have done it."

"The priest,--what priest? " "Why, the one from the little French church. Old Georges went to fetch him, but we'd had so much wine that Georges couldn't explain himself, and the priest thought there was someone sick, and came immediately. When he got there, it was just about half-past five in the morning. The room was full of bottles and tobacco-smoke, and Villefort was playing "AU the little sheep and lambs," and singing it as well. When the good father came in and saw Mimi fast asleep in an armchair and the rest of us looking as though we had been boiled in old Bordeaux, he just bolted, Paddy." "Ah," says I, "it's astonishing how the ecclesiastical mind revolts at originality. Ye couldn't call him back, Harry?" "No, I didn't try. We're to have a special license now and to be married in the morning. Mimi's sent for her clothes, and I've got a frock-coat coming over." "Will you live in this place when it's done?"

"Ah, that's what I don't know. You see, I had to catch her by a trick, but I won't keep her that way, for we have our livings to get. That's a task I must set about at once." "You were for setting about it two years ago. I remember you bought a quire of paper and two nibs, and were for writing the History of the Palais Royal. You got as far as a sketch of Cardinal Richelieu dining at the Ritz Hotel, didn't you? " "Yes, Paddy, but it's a , great scheme, and I shall finish it some day." "Some day is the Bohemian's yesterday. He's always going to do great things yester- day. Harry, my boy, you're taking the devil's own risk; there are few men who would countenance you, I suppose." "But you, you, Paddy, you don't forbid it?"

"I've wished it from the start. It may be the making of you—if it isn't the ruin. I'd sooner see you married to this little girl than dangling at a married woman's apron- strings as you were in Paris. Riches don't go for much if they can't do better than that for you, Harry." "Oh, but you're talking of things that hare been. I don't want to hear about them— heaven knows, there are sad moments enough." "Why sad moments?" "I cannot tell you. It's just obstinacy. Sometimes I tell myself that even if I marry I ahall not keep her with _met I'm

afraid of her own past, afraid of my own fu- ture. Consider what gipsy lives we have led. How are we to go on living them, how am I to hope that she will scttle down to the hum' drum things of a suburban existence? And, of course, I dare not take her back to Paris; you know how 'foolish that would be." "Put the thought out of your head. You would be a madman to play with it. As for keeping her—well, a man who cannot keep a woman- who loves him isn't worth his salt. I'll not hear it. You have no right to 1)1 talking like this—not to-night anyway. Begin to speak of dark things when the sun is set- ting on your happiness. You can keep it above the horizon RS Joshua did if you set out to .slaughter the heathen who are the masters of your idleness. Work, Harry— that's the best fiq in a man's home." _

He did not answer mc ; in truth, he had no chance. The dinner made its appearance, and we all sat down to it—such a merry coin-- pany that must have recalled all the days of the old Kit Kat Club, and of the wild dogs that- frequented the same. For you must know, Clara, that this Hampstead place baS seen the poets Keats and Leigh Hunt, who was another writing man, and Charles' Dickens, to say nothing of the prize fighters who had their training quarters in tlicsi parts, as Harry told me over the dinner table; and I'll warrant there has b.!en many such a carouse as we held this night, and with reasons not half so good. Meat and drink, song and dance—the men breaking up the chairs and tables; all sorts of musi4 wine enough to float a man of war, French ways and manners of it—ay, a night and a morning, too, for the bride fell fast asleep in the arm-chair just when the sun came up, and there were three of us on the benches. in the garden when they cried the milk in the streets. Nor- will I write this to our shame. We -were children of the highway for the nonce. God knows, there is too much of brick and mortar in the world.

You may ask me, Clara, how I, a decent man in my own country, and respected in County Wicklow—as any clergyman who plays golf will bear witness—how I can en- courage this tipsy life or give moral support to my old friend, Henry Gastonard, when he is the victim of it. I'll tell you in a word. He will go to the devil if he does not marry this little witch, and the way he has set out to marry her is the only one by which his journey's end can be reached. Think of the child's life—she who danced in the booths about Paris, she who has been a fortune hunter—God help her !—almost since she was old enough to lisp any words at all. Would such a pretty waif and stray go to a man who had red pluP breeches about him' and solid silver on his table? Would she enter a house of double doors with a marble staircase beyond? Never, I'll swear, to her life's end. He has won her through her heart, and worthily won her too. They were married this morning at ter o'clock at the French Consulate, and after- wards by the man that keeps.. the Regis- trary. .The rest of us were half asleep, but we kept it up to the end, and when we left them at ten o'clock of the night and they were alone together in the house, we stood by the window a moment to watch him kiss her very tenderly before we went down the hill to the pit where London lies. She is now his wife—God bless her pretty face ! though what their future is to be, whether a fair way in a. garden of roses or all the sorrow of the children of Alsatia is more than any man may dare to say—let ,alone your affectionate brother. : PADDY.

He was out of the room in a jiffy, and we heard him running down the stairs. For my part I made no attempt whatever to light his fire.

"Paddy," said I, "he will return with those bellows on the kalends of March. I'll give ,you fifty pounds if he comes back to- day.' Paddy would. not hear of it.

"Whit! " cried he, "d'ye mean to say we have met with a swindler? "

"Undoubtedly, and a very impudent one." "I'll never believe it. Ye do the man au injustice ; 'tis a lie I say ! " Very well, Paddy; send out for some lunch and the morning newspapers. We can soon prove it one way or the other." Poor man, he was in a fearful state, for there is no more trusting soul in all Ireland to-day than Paddy O'Connell. I need not tell you, Martha, that the man never came back. The secret of his furnace was the secret of the bundle of sticks with which hp lighted his fire. These were chemically pre- pared, and generated the gas which caused the meter to register. And so, alas' poor Paddy! There was no more sorrowful man in Hampstead than my _good friend, that night. "And who knows," he asks me triumph- antly, "that one of 'em won't take a fancy to you and make you a partner in his affairs? !Tis a thing that has happened, and not so ivonderiul. Ye have Mimi to keep, and ye may have the children. Will ye be sitting idle while she starves, Harry? Shame on ye for the thought." To which I can make no response, Martha. Idleness has caught me in its iron grip, and I am spellbound. The sunny days pass so swiftly: There is a crown of tousled hair upon my pillow when I wake • I see the one face in all the World that should be there when 1 go to my sleep at night. Mimi herself appears to live in a kind of wonderland. CHAPTER XXIII.

[Paddy O'Connell shares the news with his sister Clara.]

4, The Walk, Hampstead, London, N.W. September sth, 1905. My dear Clara,—'Twas a rough crossing I had, and it found me by no means unwilling to step from the sea to the land. But I'd be no good Irishman if I complained of a little rough water between me and the Sassenach; and so here I am and, God be good to me, in the midst of as wild A company of men as ever drank wine out of a flower vase or cooked their beef on a spirit stove. And, faith, they do drink and eat from the morn- ing until the night, and, after that, from the night to the morning again—as the garden bears witness, for I swear 'tis full already of the bottles, and beginning to be heaped up at that.

There was a man at Euston who clapped a false bag over my valise and stepped into a cab with it ; but I saw him just in time, and jumped into the cab with him. He appeared by no means pleased at this; and when the driver asked "Where to? " "Why," says 1, "to Scotland Yard." You should have seen the fellow alight, leaving me in possession of a machine to steal bags which might well be a fortune to me.

But, Clara, I am to tell you of my visit to Hampstead, where Harry is—and not of any bags at all—which I now proceed to do as well as these hilarious folks will let me, end as coherently as the madness of it makes pos- sible. You should know that I found harry in a little house on the top of a hill by, Lon- don, at a place they call Hampstead; a great, big, bare heath of a wilderness where the folks go to be happy on Sundays, .and which is large enough for the drunken ones to fall down and sleep convenient. This is the famous Hampstead Heath, wherefrom, they tell me, you can see the dome of St. Paul's and Westminster Abbey, though little of one or the other did I see, but only a great big hole full of smoke and the roofs of rail- way stations, and the factory chimneys stick- ing up above it. The house itself is a bit of a place not much bigger than a cabin on a bog. Sonic man, who has a wonderful taste for the arts, painted it the colour of the great Atlantic Ocean, and there are creepers all over the face of it, and some poor little roses that are pining for the county but will get no chance of air yet awhile. As for the interior of the place, well,, there we have Harry's wit at work, for the rascal has made it as like his little house in Paris as money and pains could do; and, as if this were not enough, he has invited over a troop of the rogues that he used to know, and filled them with good red wine until there isn't a man among them who could tell you whether he's him- self or his neighbour. But here I get ahead of the story, and that, my dear Clara, will never do.

I arrived at the house about six o'clock of the evening. No man could have mistaken the place, for a great tri-coloured nag was flying out of the bedroom window, and a crowd stood before the windows to hear the Chevalier Villefort singing the French song whico has the fine classic chorus—" Fifine, elle eat dolorcuse." When I knocked at the door, loud enough to shake the door off its binges, such a shout went up as should have brought the fire engines to the street. And R hat a rushing to the door, what cries of "Entrez—herein—kum een " what hands thrust .out to drag me- along—what a tossing up of mf bag—God help the whisky—what a pandemonium! A rat fallen among terriers would not have been so shaken to the very roots as your poor Paddy. Faith, I think that about forty of them were sitting on my chest at time, though it proved afterwards that there were but five in the house, includ- ing the little witch that Creuze should have painted, and she was as wild as any of them, and as ready for the frolic.

Well, their. pulled me into a room that was conveniently furnished with a piano that had but two or three notes to it, and chairs that had no proper backs to them; and, eeeing that I was hungry and famished after the journey, they set a bottle of 'curacoa and a yard of bread before me and bade me fall too. The place itself was so thick with smoke that I was hard put to it to say whether I was looking out of the front of my head or the back; and I was in no way surprised to hear that they had made a night and a day of it, and proposed to double the term. As for the men, their clothes would have made the fortune of a circus. Harry himself wore a suit of travelling checks loud enough to knock down a nigger minstrel. The long- whiskered beggar-man, Georges Oleander, bad an old golfer's red coat to his back and a sea-green waistcoat for its own brother; the lady-killer Villefort, a real Frenchman as you see them in Paris and a gentleman .as well, he wore a frock-coat and a rose like a cabbage in his buttonhole; while as for the little witch Mimi, she was dressed in a frock down to her knees and a pair of crimson stockings bright enough to light the candles. What it all meant—the house, the people, the noise—your Paddy knew no more than the people in the street. What was worse, no man among them seemed able to tell him.

"Finish yotir breakfast first," says Harry —it was then about half-past eix o'clock of the afternoon—"and then we can lay the cloth for dinner. I've ordered it from the confectioner's, and we're going to have a real good time. Upon my word, Paddy, you were an old brick to come—whatevcr should we have done without you?" "Why?" says I, wondering still more, Pend what do you propose to do with me?" "Why, to make you sing "Finnigan's Wake', to begin with, and then the next best song you can remember. Come, Paddy, no heel-taps—you must be thirsty,' and I wish we had something else but euracoa. They've drunk all the wine and I've sent for some more."

"From what I perceive," says I, "they have already drunk what you've sent for. Is it a wake or a wedding, Harry? You didn't send for me all the way from Ireland to join in a smoking-concert. I'll not bElieve it at all."

He said "Hush" and, presently, when the others were fallen to their games again, he took me out into the bit of a garden, where there was a fountain and a satyr—though the gentleman had a clay pipe in his mouth instead of a flute, and someone had sketched the picture of a broken bottle just where he should have worn his tail. Here we had a moment's privacy, and here I began to get at the truth of it.

"Harry," asks I, "will ye answer me a plain question—what are all these tipsy gentlemen doing here, and why have you brought that little lady among them?"

CHAPTER XXIV.

[ln which Henry Gastonard keeps hie promise to Martha Warrington.] 4, The Walk, Hampstead, N.W. September 21st, 1905.

Dear Martha,--I have owed you a letter for a long time, but really, my dear cousin; a man whose honeymoon is but a fortnight old has little time to think of the sun—and his days are brief enough. I was sorry to hear. that Arthur considers my marriage a "mere scramble on to the banks after a wild plunge into the vortex of sin." I hope he was not eating new bread and butter when he uttered this masterpiece. Marriage, I remember, was not made much of by St. Paul, and Arthur used to be a Pauline until he met you. What he is now I have not yet discovered. You, who have broken the box of sweet spices at his feet, are right to complain of the. holes in his socks— but ,as a married man I have no sympathy with you.

This is dreadful news, too, about your hair. These new dyes are troublesome tenants, and do not take our hair upon a re- pairing lease. It really was very noble of you to dye it so bright a red for the sake of the Pageant. And now, you say :that the dye won't come out, and that you must return to Beldon still wearing the brand of Boadicea Cheer up, Martha. Have not some of the noblest women in history—chief amongst them our Elizabeth of blessed memory— dressed auburn locks for posterity and gloried in ,their possessions? For my part, had I known of the fact when writing my skit, "The People of the Pageant," I would have mentigned it to your lasting honour. The little book appears to be getting about— I had no idea that such a trifle could intercst so many. But concerning more serious things. • I am living, as you wished me to live, in a box of a cottage upon Hampstead Heath. The place is pretty enough, and DOW that we are married I am putting some comfort into it. This is only to be done secretly and by stealth. Chairs, Which were not there yester- night, arc discovered at breakfast time. A new piano dropped from the heavens, so to speak, and has notes that play. I have bought' a splendid brass bed, and the men rigged it up while Mimi was out shopping. She sus- pects me, but says little. 1 have not told her in the very word that I: am poor;: but have led her to that belief, ond her devotion is the consequence. Would' it be , foolish to tell you, Martha, that I am not wholly happy in spite Of an this? The vaguest fears afflict me. I know not from day to day what evil is to overtake me, and yOt lam conscious of evil. Per- chance it is but the aftermath of golden days, lived in a sunshine I had never hoped to see. Perhaps it is but a lover's humour—l cannot say, and yet it is as real as any thought that ever dwelt with me.

You must know that o:d Paddy O'Connell, the wild Irishman with the thunderous voice and the jet black locks and the magnificent figure, remains, gcod friend that he is, in London -to "see me through it," whatever that may mean. He is lodged at the Jack Straw Castle Inn, on the very summit of UK Heath here, and he is with us the best part of the day, and often the best part of the night As Mimi refuses (because of my
