CO-OPERATIVE EXHIBITION.

MR. F. MADDISON M.P. REPLIES

TO CRITICISMS

The Co-operative exhibition in the Parr Hall was brought to a close on Saturday evening, after a very successful week. Throughout the evening the hall was thronged with people and the various exhibits were inspected with great interest. At seven o'clock a short meeting was held, when Mr. F. Maddison, M.P. for Burnley, delivered an address in which he replied to criticisms of the movement contained in a handbill which had been freely circulated amongst visitors to the hall.

Mr. Nathan Cook, who presided, said they could congratulate themselves upon having had an excellent exhibition, which spoke volumes for the progress of the Movement. A point which he wished to emphasise was that all the goods were made under proper conditions of labour. There was no sweating. When any of the committee had gone round the wholesale works and had asked the employes whether they were satisfied with the conditions, there had not in one single instance been a word of com- plaint. It was now nearly fifty years since the Society was established in Warrington. Several anti-Co-operative pamphlets had been issued that day • whether the Society had stirred up some of the traders in Warrington or not he did not know. Some of the statements contained in them were not true and he took particular exception to one of them to the effect that a man could not be a co-operator and a trade unionist. To his mind, co-operation and trades organizations ran very closely together and he as a believer in both was not going to sacrifice mlher his trade unionism or his co-opera- tive principles. (Applause.) Mr. F. vl ADDISON, who hat a cordial reception, said he had prepared an address on "Co-operation in relation to the problem of poverty" but when he arrived in the teen he had placed in his hand a bill headed "Reasons why I should not be a Ce-operat That was rather neigh en him—(laughter)—and it eppeared to him desirable that he should devote his address to some of the fallacies—and they were many— contained in the bill He wished them clearly to under- stand,for his own sake and that of the movement, that he came there not as party politician. He had been to War- rington in that capacity and might come again, but a Co-operative platform was not the place to advocate party politics whether it be Socialism, Tor} ism or Liberalism, the latter being the party to which he belonged. Co-operators pushed their societies not bee ruse they were politicians, but because they believed it was to the advantage of themselves and their families to trade and belong to Co-operative societies. They must not close their door by any political test. Once they did that, they destroyed this great movement. It stood wide open now for all parties and for none •, the societies did not interfere with their members' politics and they ought not to allow the movement to be interfered with by politics. That exhibition was for the promotion of voluntary co-operation in production and distribution Alluding to the h+ndbill Mr. Maddison remarked "I venture to say that the average tradesman in Warring- ton has no more to do with the issue of a bill like this than E have. The people who are behind the movement are a bigger nuisance to the private trader than they are to the Co-operative Society, for they never hurt us." The bill stated "I should not be a co-operator, because I derive my living from individual enter- prise ; trading and co-operation has declared its object to be the destruction of the means whereby I live." He had only one objection to that—the trifling one that it was not true. (Laughter). In the first place, what was meant by individual enterprise? Any intelligent man knew that one spoke of individual enterorise as opposed to State enterprise. H (Mr. Maddison) was opposed to State Socialism. Just imagine him coming to Warrington to take put in a movement which, according to the circular, was in the interests- of State ownership rather than individual enterprise! Mr. Maddison pointed out that a huge concern like the London and North-western Railway Company did not cease to be individual enterprise because there was more than one man in it. Every penny of the capital in co-operative societies was sub- scribed by individuals, just as the money in the rail- way c ,mpany was subscribed by individuals. There- fore the statement in the circular was absolutely and entirely incorrect and showed stupidity and ignorance of ordinary terms. The bill contained the dastardly insinuation : "Because if I refuse to support that trade which employs me, my employers will have a perfect right to refuse to give me employment." That represented a very despicable spirit and he ventured to say that not one of the great firms which had made the name of Warrington so well known would endorse such a sentiment. Taking the great soap firm of Crosfield—and he did not want to bring in Mr. Arthur Crosfield as a politician at all—did they believe for a moment that the firm would think of making it a condition of employment that a man should not it vest his own money in his own way and buy his own goods wherever he liked? Tyranny was not altogether dead but it had not survived in such a bald form as the stupid person who wrote that circular set med to imagine. He (the speaker) had been making a few inquiries about the attitude of the Co-operative Society in Warrington to other firms and he found that the Society had money invested in Crosfleld's. Just think of a man attempting to incite employers against Co-operators in a town where the workmen who formed the Co-operative Society had actually invested come of their own money in a firm which these people were trying to incite to refuse men employment because they were Co-operators. The circular further stated that they should not be Co-operators "Because Co-operativism is the cause of much unemployment, and it is the duty of all workers not to support a move- ment which has that effect." That was bad economics. They must always trade in the best., most, organized, and most scientific fashion, and because Co-operation often stopped a great deal of waste and gave a greater oppor- tunity of buying in the best market, the workers' busi- ness was to do that which was hest in trade, and if they did that they gave the mcst employment all round. What was saved in cne direction was invested and employed in other directions, and Mr. Maddison instanced the effect which the change from the stage coach to the modern railway had had in opening up fresh fields of employment. This circular belonged to the economics of 500 years ago. Another reason given was "Because I can have my wants better supplied by traders than by the' Co-operative Stores." If so, let them go to the traders. Ho was not • an orthodox Co-operator—or an orthodox anything— (laughter)—and he said that if they could prove that the private traders gave them better advantages,all things considered, they must go to the private traders. Co-operators claimed that Co-operation had provided a great want and it would lire or die just in proportion as it Continued to do so. Their business was not to blackguard private traders; they had as much right to open their shops as Co-operators had to open tbeir stores, and they had a perfect right to the profit which the puslic give them by their purchases, end to do exactly as they liked, so long as they acted honourably and within .the law. Co-operators however, claimed from the private traders exactly wit they gave. Work- men having earned their money must be absolutely free to spend it as they liked and if they were not allowed to do that, it vas introducieg the Truck system again. The circular went on to state as another reason: "Because the high rate of interest paid to those Co-operators who have money invested is taken out of the increased price put on to the goods." -Economistshad recognized that it was the Co-operative movement which had done more than any other effort to reduce high rates of interest on capital. It was the one great feature of the Co-operative movement. The shareholders in the Warrington Co-operative Society who purchased no goods at all only received 21 per cent. and the highest rate of interest paid was a little over 4 per cent. Another argument brought forward was: "Because such a large number of Co-operative Societies have ended disastrously, with ruinous results to the confiding members." It was quite true that Co-operative Societies had failed and it was absolutely true that they -would fail in the future, but what about the private traders? Had there been no failures of private traders in the past? Did they know nothing of Liberator Societies, of Whittaker Wright companies and of other concerns which were in the papers every day ? To accu,se the movement of having failed in some of its societies and to urge that as a reason why people should not be Ca-operators was so utterly childish AS not, to deserve even a word of comment. The real fact was that there was nothing more marvellous than the comparatively few distributive societies, having regard to the circumstances, which had failed since the

movement began If they took this average of the last les years rind co.. plied it with private traders, the haloes could net be aeairisi Ce-operators. The circularities stated " Because the G.-operative Societies themselves are trusts of a very serious character." That. ire" compliment to the movement, for Co-operation u° doubt was very serious to trades who were " sufficiently well eqiiipped to withstand the competrtiou of Co-operative Societies. The essence of a trustr however, w.is that it was a close corporation what wt's the feature of Co-operative Societies? By tbe verY Act under %hid] they were registered, they threw theft; dcors open to every reasonable pers. n who woni become a member. They were exactly what a trust log not. Where:is the trust was orgauierd for the produ?es u as against the consumer the distributive C.-operatil Society was formed of consumers whose great Ana vital interest wr uld be to protect themselveti; "Co-operators' pretensiors of philanthropy etc " state!' the circular "are a delusion, and they do not pay tbel fair share towards the maintenance of the State. Ninety-five per cent of the membership cf tbo Co-operative Societies of the kingdom were ordinere wage-earning workmen. They welcomed everybody but be hoped the movement would always remain mainly s workingclass movement. The glory of it was that whatever success had attended its efforts had bee° achieved by the faithfulness and loyalty and eoefideo9° of the working classes, and they were proud of I'. (Applause). To talk of these men and women shout contributing to the maintenance of the State might be all right as a p'ece of hiunrar, but as anything else it was absurd. It was said that 1 °cause the 9,000 ntent• hers of the Warrington Co-oie naive Society nrigh,l- keep many small shops going, there were le,s rates eat to the town. So there might be, but what about the great multiple shops that came into the town? All the money that ran them was mainit out of the town, but the distinctive feature er the C3-operative S vciety was that incurl was local. If they objected to a scientific form of business ancN• to a reduction of woe, then they must oblject to the great company combine' tions who never spent a penny more in manrigeineut than was absolutely necessary. And it was quite right. How would the memkers of the Co-operative SocietY be better off with four times as many premises it the shops they alioady hid were sufficient? Anything above that would be caste, and everything wasted vrculd add to the price hut notsto the value of the goods sold. The people who were responsible for the circular were at war with scientific industry and vrould substitute the old blundering methods of 100 years age for that which was up-to-date and had proved its v 53.110 in this nation. It was said that the Co-operatrve Society gave 'lathing to philanthropy. He vrsi informed that the Warringt -in Society contributed t) the Infirmary and Nurses' Home and to the relief oc the unemployed. But even more important than that wes the fact that the Society cont raeit ed one per cent.every year from its profits to the education not only of its members hut of non-ruemb-rs if they would trouble to come to 1113 meetings and lectures. Thera were rnstll Societies which spelt 21 per cant. on the work—il wished the Warringten Society did—and to accuse tleo great working c'ass movement, composed of men with comparatively snail wages, of meanness in their generosity was not only shabby but silly. (Applause.) As a metter of fact before free libraries existed as theY did now the Co-o,perat ive Society in many places was doing the work of the technical and night school. As educated democracy was the only means by which the wrekers could come into their rightful inheritance. (Applause.) He had said nothing of that pin of the movement which to him was the mor,. important of the two—he eant labour co-parteership by which they.!: only sought to distribute goods but to produce them under the best possible conditions. Great thing had been dere arid greiter things would ne done in the future In all their workshops trade union con- ditions were ob-rerved. They bad done in that move' ment what legislation would never acoomplish--. although it might do rennething —eliminated sweating(Applause). That was prate ical politics—he menet industrial politics, not the otherstuff. (Laughter). And that was the appeal he would make to his trade unionist friends It was all very well going about denouncing capitalists until they were black in tbe face. That was an easy thing to do, hut it did not do much good as a rule. It was, however, quite another thine to spend their wages without the slightest regard for the way in which their goods were produced. I..et tnem put their trade unionism into their spending. (Applaese). It was an easy thing to talk about the brotherhood of humanity and then go home end - treat one's wife badly. He did not believe I." that internetionalism. (Laughter) Some of hut friends could paint a lovely piettre of the social millennium. It might be right; he had no poetic imaeination himself. (Lau4hter.) What he said was: Begin now, begin in Warrington nni try to build pert of the wall which would make strong the social and industrial city of the future. (Applause.) '1 hey could do it by spreading the principles of co-partnership which demanded thet not only should trade union rages be paid, but that there should be a participation in the profits that ware the result of labour. He believed that the future of the social betterment of the working classes lay very little in Acts of Parliament but depended much more largely upon a divine disootr tent that would make the workmen not satisfied with the mere wage system. The time had come when they must be more than ordinary wage emitters. They must also be partners in industry and they of the Labolt Co-partnership Association wanted that principle 13 workmen's societies and in private concerns so that everywhere the worker should be imbued with the desire for a larger amount of property than he possessed. Of all the movements he knew, there was none which surpassed the Co-operatiye movement in the distribu- tion of wealth. (Applause). On the motion of Mr. W. Harem, seconded by Mr. G. T. RUSHTON, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Maddison. During the evening the Borough Band played capital selections.

Lymm. APPOINTMENT FOR A WARRINGTON TEACHER.—At a meeVng of the Administrative Education Sub-Com- mittee of the Cheshire County Council on Thursday night the appointment of Miss Annie Bowyer as assistant mistress under Miss Thomley, at Oughtrington school was confirmed.

INDEPENDENT METHODIST CHAPEL—The anniversary sermons were preached at this place of worship on Sunday, when Mr. J. Whitley, of Stockton Heath, preached to large congregations. In the afternoon there was a service of song entitled "Samuel," Mr. Waterworth giving the connective readings.

PARISH CHURCH CHOIR CONCERT.—A successful concert in aid of the Mods of the Lymm Parish Church Choir was held on Wednesday night in the Drill Hall. The arrangements were in the hands of Mr. VS illiam Crooke, organist of the Church, who had succeeded in getting together a company whose efforts were of a high order and much appreciated by the audience. Mr. Cooke acted as accompanist, whilst the role of humorist was sustained by Mr. Frank B. Heydon. POLICE COURT.—On Monday last at the Police Court before Mr. Alfred Watkin and Mr. S. W. Gould, John Caldwell who stated that he Came from Manchester where he had been employed at the Midland Hotel, was charged with begging at Miss Fox's and Mr. J. Palmer's, Brookfield. He was remanded until Tuesday when the investigations of the police having proved his story to be false he was sentenced to-14 days imprisonment. A similar case was also dealt with on Tuesday, the prisoner being sentenced to seven days imprisonment. MAY QUEEN.—Mr. E. Pickavance of Warrington and Mr. T. Gregson of Lymm have been appointed judges of the cyclists parade. The apaointment of judge for the tradesmens turnout is being held over, Mr. S. W. Gould who was asked to officiate, being unable to undertake the task as he will be away on the date of the festival —3rd June. The question of insuring against loss through rain has been engaging the close attention of the committee and at their meeting • on Tuesday it was decided to take out a policy. THE CROSS.—The protest against the threatened desecration of the ancient cross and its surroundings by the fixing of poles to carry telephone and telegraph wires direotly over the cross, has been successful. Mr. F. E. Evans the hon.. secretary of the Tradesmen's Association having received a communication from the Postmaster General stating that the difficulty would be overcome by altering the position of one of the poles. The thanks, not only of the community but of all those who from surrounding districts find pleasure in a visit to this ancient and picturesque Cheshire village, are due to those who by their pi ompt action prevented the wires being fixed in the position originally intended. I.YitiM CROSS.

The Clerk now read a letter from the Rector in which he stated he had received a letter from the General Post Office in answer to a resolution forwarded from the vestry meeting. He believed that Mr. Evans bad had a similar letter. As the Council met on Thurs- day it would be hardly worth troubling the Church to give a decision. He hoped to be present on Thursday and if the matter was brought-npthen it would receive attention by the Press and misgivings would thereby be allayed. _ _

The Clerk also read a letter from the General Post Office, London, dated April 26th. To Mr. Frederick E.' Evans: Sir, with reference to your letter of the 17th inst. I am directed by the Postmaster-General to say that neither the route in question nor the positions of the poks were decided by the Post Office engineer arbitarily but-were chosen by him in consultation with representatives of the Lynun Urban District Council, and if it is considered that there is any objection to the wires being carried over the villgge Cross, it is unfortunate th t this was not represented when the preliminary atrongements were being made. It is undesirable to alter the line es its stability must thereby suffer to some extent and in any case no alteration can Ise made without the consent of the Urban District Council. If, however, the Council's consent can be obtained without delay, the alteration may be carried out before the working party leaves the neighbourhood provided that the consent of a private owner to the !I placing of a stay on his land be obtained should this be found necesslry. After a short discussion on the proposition of Coun- cillor Welford oconded by Councillor Moston, the following resolution was passed : "Whilst disclaiming all responsibility for the posi; ion of the existing pole about which complaint is made, this Council consents to the fixing of such a pole on the footpath on the opposite side of the Ycad.'

• WHIST DRIVE AND D.tsc E.—A V% hist drive and dance in connection with the Sunday School was held in the Church schools, Padgate, on Thursday evening. Mr. Z. PADGATE WESLEYAN CHOI‘IL—A grand "At Home" and" Gipsy" cancert (in aid of the choir funds) was given in the Wesleyan Schoolroom, Padgate, on Thurs- day evening, Mr. Johnston (the choirmaster) occupying the chair. The schoolroom presented a pleasing spectacle being gaily decorated with bunting, flowers and plants, and great praise is due to the members of the choir who had so kindly given their time and ser- vices to the work of decbrating. Another striking feature of the concert was the seating arrangements. Small square tables were scatte:ed about the room and around those the audience was seated giving to the concert a free and easy at home kind of look very rarely seen at concerts of this description. In cue corner of the room was the usual gip3y tripod and cauldron' near to which wit. 3 a gipsy tent inhabited by lady members of the choir, most of whom were very prettily dressed as gipsies. Refreshments kindly given by members of the choir and friends were partaken Of during the interval. Competitions of unique interest were held during the evening, one of which .was a whistling competition for ladies in which the follow- ing took part : Mrs. Dutton and Mrs. A. Ward, Misses J. Ward, 13. England, L. Duckworth, M. Bouchier, and L. Word. The competition for the prize was very keen, many of the ladies proving to be adepts in the whistling art. A tie was declared between three of • the corn- petitoi s, and after another keen struggle the prize was very deservedly awarded to Miss J. Ward. Another com- peitien which created much amusement was impromptu speeches for gentlemen, in which the following touk part :—Messrs. B. Mason, S. Adamson, E. Overton, W. J. Rite, J. R. Fletcher, Dorton, Plinston and Duckworth. The judge, 'in awarding the prize to Mr. Dutton for his excellent speeekon" Should bachelors be taxed " also commended Mr. E. Overton for his speech on " Singing " and Mr. Duckworth for iris skilful handl;ng of, the Suffragette question. Mr. J. R. Fletcher, of Colcheth. acted as judge for the ladies' whistling competition and the Rev. T. Cladding for the impromptu speeches. The prizes rove:v:4 were very kindly given by Mr. G. White" one of the choir rnernbeis. A capital muileal programme was gone through. Part songs entitled " Gtpsies we" and "Gipsy Girls" were very ably rendered by the ladies of the choir. Much praise is due to Mrs. Haviour who, gave a splendid *exhibition of violin play ing, her first solo "Romance" by . Suendi or being rewarded by an encore, for which she contributed ilandel's "Largo." She was also equally successful in the second half of the programme, Danda's "Romance and Bolero" being rendered with much feeling. Equally enjoy-able were the beautiful monologues of Miss 'Carney. She gave quite a dramatic rendering to "A lesson to husbands" and "A little quaker sinner" being rewarded for the latter by a well deserved encore. The song " Esinerelda " by Mrs. Dutton and "Noon- tide Lullaby" by Miss Olive Gladding were exquisitely sung and 'a humorous duet entitled "Where are 3ou going to my pretty maid" by Miss L. Ward and W. J. Bate added Much to the Inte-e3t of the evening. Mr. J. R. Fletcher was .very .lourlly applauded for his singing of" Dinah, she said Yah" and the skilftil handling of the " bones " in a hones solo, being recilled for an encore for each item. A pianoforte solo was given .by Mr. A. Domville who also acted as accompanist along with Miss L. Ward and Miss Gladding. The concert concluded with the sing- ing of a humorous quartette entitled "A very bad cold" the audience joining at the finish in the National Anthem. The proceeds realized about 2110 s.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WIDNES MAN'S BAD RECORD.

James Broadhurst was charged at the Widnes Police Court on Monday with having stolen two pairs of trousers, the property of Frederick Neil, pawnbroker, of Ann-street West, Widnes. From the evidence it transpired the prisoner was seen to take the goods fi-om a rail inside the shop by a.ten-year-old boy named Anthony Kay, on Saturday evening last, about a quarter past eight. He was aftersards seen to go up an entry at the rear of Mr. Neil's shop. Prisoner had a record of forty-five previous convictions, and was now scut to gaol for three months' hard labour. THE TREATMENT OF CONSUMPTION.

The committ,oe appointed by the North-Western Poor Law Conference in September last to inquire into the best means by combination -of Unions or otherwise of providing special tr9atment of consumptive cases reported that no such combination could possibly be brought about otherwise than upon the intimation of Unions which were desirous of combining with the consent of the Local Government Board. Combinations suggested by third parties would have but slight chance of meeting with the approval of the Unions primarily interefted.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

AIR. SEDDON AND POSTMAN'S WAGES.

Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., speaking in the debate on postal matters in the House of Commons on Tuesday. said he objected to the cheeseparing policy which had been adopted under the Hobhouse report. The injustice in the minds of those who were suffering was keen and widespread. Out of 900 cases, in something like 800 there had been a reduction v. ithorit any inquiry. For the sake of the public service and contentment in the Postal Department, he hoped the Postmaster-GenerJl would give his attention to the matter. No man ought to be compelled to live in a district on a low wage. He had made inquiry in Earlestown. St. Helens and Liverpool, and he found that a man in Liverpool could live if not cheaper, quite as cheaply as as men in St. Helens and Earlestown. If that was so, then a case had been made out that reduction ought not to have taken place ,without inquiry. In many cases where inquiry had taken place it had been carried out in a haphhzard manner. Because the official making the inquiry happened to hit upon a trader who WS g known as a cutter in the trade selling second-grade articles, the postmen had to suffer reduction. Ile appealed to the right hon. gentlemen to remove the cause of unrest. It was because of that unrest that so many protests had reached lign. members. Until an inquiry was held no reduction should take place.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
PARENTS AND SON AT LAW.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Sbiress Will, K.C., Thomas and Alice Pope, 8, Princes-street, Earlestown, brought an action against theft son,,Rober t Pope, 9, Stanley-street, Earles- town, for specific performance, of an agreement for the sale of the business of a b'acltsmith, or in the alterna- tive £lO3 damages.—Mr. H. J. L?ngton appeared for the plaintiffs and Mr.A. Browne repesented the defendant.— For the plaintiffs it was stated that they agreed to sell to 63 defendant, and the defendant agreed to purchase a blacksmith's shop, stable and coke. yard, including a steam hammer, an engine boiler' an iron rack and an office in King-street and Princess-street, Earlestown' to which the plaietiffs were entitled as leaseholders, for thesum of £O3 subject to a mortgage for £3lO at 5 per cent. and the ground rent of £lO 17s. The agree- ment was contained in a letter dated 17th, 1909 addressed to the plaintiffs' agent, Mr. Longton, and signed by the defendant, offering to purchase the premiecs and property at £lO3, subject to the mortgage, and in a telegram dated Marct} 13th, addressed to defendant, and Mr. Longton accepted the offer. Defen- dant, however, refused to perform the agreement, hence the prestnt action.—Tn the course of larr evidence Mr. Browne said his defence was that the sum of £lO3 was tor stock and not for equity.--Mrs. Pope said the business had gone to rack ani ruin since the defendant left. She admitted that s'le was short of money.—Mr. Brown canteaded that. the contract Was signed in a mistake. They k tew ,nothing of the pay- ment of the money by instalments. lie submitted there was no cant rect.—Mr. Longton eald the matter of the payment of the purchase had nothing to do with the contract. The contract was a specific one, the defendant offering £103! He pointed out that the payment of the money by instalments nas to the advantage of the defendant.—His Honour held that the alleged contract was not a contract authorized by the plaintiffs, and gave judgment for the defendant with costs.

NEWTON CONSERVATISM. TORY LORDS AND THE GOVERNMENT.

The annual meeting of the Newton Division Conserva- tive Association was held at the Newton-le-Willows Town Hall, on Saturday, Lord Newton presiding over a good attendance. Lord Newton was re-elected presi- dent. Lord Wolmer, addressing the meeting, said that they were unfortunate in having no unpopular Government to represent this country, and it was being proved at by-election after by-election how much the country was against them. With reference to the dis- establishment of the Church in Wales, he asked what were they to say of a Government that appointed a Commission to inquire into the state of the Church in that country, and when they found that the Com- missiun was going to report against them brought in a bill befere the report was received? The Churoh in Wales, declared the speaker, was performing a mag- nificent work and using its powers to the utmost. No Church was in a more flourishing condition, and it , would be nothing less than confiscation to take money which was used for religious purposes and spend it for secular purposes. Referring to the fiscal policy, Lord Wollner said tho system could not last much longer, and he firmly believed that at the next general election, the country would endorse that belief. The Government. stated that they had the wish of tbe people, but they refrained from putting it to the test; while they also stated they would not sit down under the_ rule of the House of Lon's, but as soon as the Lords did a thing they took it lying down. With rega.rd to the Labour party, ho said the dissension in their ranks was not likely to Inspire the country with confidence in them. Lord Newton criticised Mr. Asquith on the national defence question. It was useless be said, to disguise from themselves the fact that Germany was trying to oust Britain from her naval supremacy. It had been said that the German and English democracies were so fond of each other that there was no fear of anything from them. For his own part, he was sure that unless things altered a contest was as inevitable between them as was a contest between Mr. Seddon and Lord Weimer. It was said they should build two ships to Germany's one, but that did not meet with his view. If they were going to do that, it was going to cost a lot of money, and Germany was a rich nation. He was not at all sure that any good purpose would be served. Let them keep ahead of Germany as long as they could, but, at the same time, they must not neglect their military. The disestablishment of the Church in -Wales, he declared, was nothing but a bribe to the Noncon- formists of England and Wales, and, the speaker added, what would be the result of the Budget but a bribe to come other portion of the community.

T. HELENS.

HUGE PENNY SUBSCRIPTION. The report of the St. Helens Hospital for the past year F. hows a great expansion in the work of the institution, 1,070 patients having been in the hospital 30,992 days, as against in the previous year 814 patients for 28,581 days. The income for the year has heen £5,106 158.83., which enables the committee to put a balance of £174 12s. to the existing balance of £287 ss. 93. Of this, £lOO is to be transferred to form the nucleus of a further enlargement fund. The workmen of the town have during the year given £3,723 lie. 6d. in the penny a week subscription to the hospital. - ST. HELENS MUSICAL SOCIETY. The St. Helens Musical Society, which has been evolved out of the St. Helens Glee Club, held its annual spring concert on Monday night, at the Theatre Royal. The society has succeeded in a comparatively short time in establishing a reputation not only for most excellent chorus work, but also for instrumental num- bers of exceptional interest. The orchestra, under Mr. Gordon E. Stutely, last night scored a great success. Mr. Thomas Southwortb, solo viol,n,played the famous "Concerto in G Minor," by Max Bruch, and Tschaik- owsky's " 1812" overture was submitted by special rcquest. The chores, aided by Mr. John 13ardsley,tenor rendered Standford's " Revenge " and "Hiawatha's Wedding Feast" in most capable fashion, and, with Dr. Siddall, the conductor 2 received a flattering ovation from the crowded audience.

Brighouse, inquired into the death of George Mason, 41, and his son John, eight, who wet° found dead in their house in Morley-street last Friday. The wife gave evidence that she left her husband three months ago after a quarrel about money matters. Other witnesses said the man had been depressed for some time. When the police entered the house they found the man and his son lying on a bed. The father's head was enveloped in a pillow slip, under which was the end of an indiarubber tube attched to the gas braaet. Mr. Davies said that round the man's neck was a scarf tied tightly-with a double tmnanda running knot in the frontt On removing the scarf he found a wound on the &el,' The man had evidently attempted to cut his throat, but his death was due to coal gas poisoning. Round the neck of the boy there was a scarf loosely tried, but a post-mortem examination bad shown that in the boy's case, too, gas and not strangulation was the cause of death.

TAXATION OF LAND VALUES

Where The Polley Has Been Tried. The people of New Zealand have a tax on land values and they have frequently testified that this tax has stimulated the building trade. It has done this by destroying, more or less completely, one part of the land companies' business, that part which the Colonials \facetiously call "the vacant lot industry," or in other words, the holding up of land against builders. _ _

The Town Clerk of Wellington, the capital of New Zealand, 1902-3, reported "The rEsult of the first year's trial of this system of rating must be considered a gratifying one, and haves no room for regret at its' adoption. • . It is only stet ing a fact ta sly that much. if not all, of the nativity in the building (.prat ions of the city and surroundings during the phst year is due to the 'influence of this healthy measure.'" _

In a subsequent th report e Town Clerk said i : ," It s worthy of nite that there are now more than 20 Boroughs in New Zealand which have adopted the Land Values Taxation System, a fact which at least shows that the principle is finding favour among our own peop!e." RESULTS OF THE PoLicr.—Fonowing on these statements came the more practical testimony of thel facts of the case. The" New Zealand Dairyman "for October, 1905, reported " A tremendous amount of building is going on in Wellington just now, the amount of capital involved exceeding a third of a million. 'Dile D.I.C. building is to cost £40,000, and the value of the almost com- pleted Bank of New South Wales building is £35,000. The erection of now hotels is not so popular as wAs the case a little over a year ago. '1 he only hotel being re-erected is the Western. It is of six storeys, and will cost £25,000. Victor College is still an or construction, and is to cost £25,090. A substantial building is being erected for the Bank of Australasia at a cost of £34,000. The plans have almost been completed for the New National Bank, and the Union ; ;Bank is to be enlarged: A new Public Trust Office is to be built at a cost of £20,010. A warehouse, to cost £20,000, is being erected for Messrs. P. Hayman and Co., and another for Messrs. E. W. Mills and Co., to cost £16,000. A convent is in course of erection at Island Bay for £15,000, and the same amount is involved in the new fruit and produce market. for which the plans are completed. The Wellington Building and Investment Company are erecting premises worth £15,000, -and a private hotel, nearly finished, touches the same figure. A block of shops and dwelling-houses is being erected for Mr. Martin Kennedy at a cost of £ll,OOO. Mr. C. H. Izard is building premises priced at £ll,OOO, and there are a great many other buildings projected, or in course of construction, involving sums of from £2,000 to £8,000."

A Veteran's Life Story.

Sir,—lt was with the greatest delight I read in your valuable paper of the 24th in 4, the life story of Mr. H. B. Bowers of, the 64th Regiment. I also have served continuously from 1885 to 1901 with that same regiment, during the whole period of its tour of foreign service in Natal, Zululand, Capetown, Mauritius, Malta, Egypt, Soudan and India, and there are two more of my comrades living at Thelwall (one of them Richardson, comes of a 64th family) who also wt,uld read Mr. Bowers' career with the greatest of interest. I have already had the pleasure of meeting Colour- Sergeant Collins of the Grenadier Company of Mr. Bowers' days and his account of the receipt of the views of the massacre at Cawnpote was one that made us all proud of the 64th, the grim words which went the rounds of the men in camp " Remember Cawnpore " and, the fury with which they attacked Nana Sahib the following morn- ing fully avenging the atrocities of the previous day. His words are still fresh in my memory. I should be proud to meet my comrade (Mr Bowers) as I am a pensioned colour-sergeant of the good old 64th too, having retired four years ago with an exemplary character, as he has formed indeed a link with the past, of a Tegirnent of whom I am proud. I fought against Dinizula in 1888,and the Dervishes in 1896, holding the medals for the last campaign (none were granted for the former 1888) and helped to put one more" Honour" on the colout s of the 0151 Blth--Yours etc,, 34, Catherine-street, Bewsey.

JAMES PARRY,

===== 0002601_19090501_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
Temperance and Employment

Sir,—l have followed with a considerable amain of interest the controversy which has been raging in your columns for the last three months and have been content to leave it to the two principals, feeling sure that they possessed more ability for dealing with the subject under discussion than myself. But, seeing that H. S. has taken upon himself the task of helping his lame friend Mr. Parker over the stile, I hope that you will give me this opportunity of reply- ing to his ,contentions. 11. S. says that he believes in the Nationalisationof some industries but not all, but surely if it is a good thing that some of our industries should be under national control, why not all? Then again H. S. says, 'That he agrees with the ultimate 'aim of Socialism which is the brotherhood of man, but Socialism would only be possible -when the brotherhood was an accomplished fact" to which it would. be as well to retort that if the brotherhood was an accomplished fact Socialism would be unnecessary. It is because we Who are Socialists realise that there is a force which under our existing social order prevents the brotherhooe of man, for it is not the man who would act honestly towards , his fellows who leads the way under our present social system, but the mean, unscrupulous ,man, the sweater who grinds his workers down to the lowest possible depths in order that he may place his goods on the markets at a lower price and so beat other com- petitors. The-would-be honest man is compelled to follow in the footsteps of these men in order that he and those who are dependent upon him may live, for we must not forget the fact that we are living under a system which compels us to buy in the cheapest and sell in the dearest markets and as cheap labour and cheap material are essential in order that our industrial magnates may gather together enormous fortunes, we believe that by nationalising industry and working-it for the good of all insteadiof for the good of a few, we are working out in a prac- tical nianner the brotherhood and sisterhood of men and women. I agree with H.S. that so long as it is in the hands of private persons to sell drink for profit, the ectucation and regeneration of not only the work- ing classes, but of -all classes will be -difficult in the extreme, but although I am a teetotaler and a member of the Independent Order of ReChabites, I can clearly see that the whole Social problem does not lie at the bottom of a glass of poisoned water. There are such questions as theadulteration of food-stuffs, insanitary conditions of labour,the housing question,female labour, and child labour; all these questions have to be con- sidered before we can discover the forces which we are aiming at the degeneration of the people." But" says ILS. " What is wanted is a scheme to limit the employer's power to oppress his workmen for pur- poses of private gain without abolishing their ...rela- tions altogether," which is equivalent to saying " Let the owning classes still oppress the workers, but not to the same extent; let the wage slave still be a wage slave, but a little less of a wage slave." But what we as Socialists say is "Abolish the whole systein which allows men to oppress their fellows, and establish a system based upon justice instead of /injustice, love instead of hate, and then the Brotherhood of man ' will be nearer than it has ever been before."—Thanking you in anticipation, I am yours

j. C. W,

IRONWORKERS AND TARIFF

REFORM.

Forthcoming Local Debate.

A debate on the interesting subject " Will Tariff Reform be to the advantage of the iron and steel workers of this country ? " btween Councillor James /Broadhurst (representing the Warrington Conservative Labour Party) and Mr. Joseph Elwell (representing the Warrington Progressive Labour League) will be held in the Thewlis-street schools on Monday evening, May 10th. It has been arranged that the opening speeches shall occupy twenty minutes each, with two following speeches each of fifteen minutes duration, the total time allowed for the debate thus being one hour, forty minutes. Councillor Broadhurst will open the debate in the affirmative. All the, time taken up by interruptions from the audience is to be allowed to each speaker and another condition is that all books and 'documents used during the debate shall be at the disposal of each disputant. No " absent " author is to be quoted and no new Matter can be introduced•into the last speeches. No vote will be taken. Tickets may be obtained .from Mr. J. Howes, jun., hon. secretary of the Progressive Labour League, and Mr. Herbert Hayes, Conservative Club. Early application is advisable as the number is limited to 200 for each side.

FIRST TOTAL ABSTINENCE SOCIETY.

STOCKTON HEATH MEMORIAL

UNVEILED. Speech by Mr. 11. J. King, M.P.

The unique distinction which Stockton Heath possesses of being the home of the first total abstin- ence- society formed in this country, -was fittingly recognized on Wednesday afternoon when Mrs. James Dodds, President of the Warrington Band of Hope Union, unveiled a memorial tablet in the old Providence schoolroom, Chapel-lane, where the society , came into being. The tablet is inscribed as follows : —"ln this building,. on Aprfl. 4th, *lB3O, the first English total abstinence society was formed, unier the advocacy of George Harrison Birkett, of Dublin, Thomas Eaton, of Stockton Heath, William Wood, of Manchester, and William Clarke, of Stockton Heath." The unveiling proceedings were combined with the opening of a bazaar in aid of the Independent Metho- dist Church and were largely attended, the little schoolroom being, filled to• its utmost capecity. Mr. A. J. King. M.P. for the Knutsford Division, made a Special journey from Lon'don to preside and was accompanied on the platform by the Revs. C. Harvey Cook and J. B. Bugloss, Mr. and Mrs. James Dodds, Mr: and Mrs. C. E. Parker, Mr. A. Mounfieldi Mr. J. Whitley, Mr. J. Longland, and Mr. W. Wylie. Mr. King remarked in the first place upon the objects of bazaars which, he said, were generally two-fold--to bring together in associated effort the friends of some cause, and to raise funds for the support and mainten- ance of that cause. He hoped that in both these aims that bazaar would be attended with ample success. Bazaars were a matter of every-day occurrence, but it was not every place or community that could claim the honour which the memorial tablet to be unveiled by Mrs. Dodds would claim for Stockton Heath and for the Independent Methodist community. (Applause.) He had read the little book which Mr. Mounfleld kindly scat him with very great interest, and he learned from it that it was in that beim' that the first total abstinence society in the country was formed. It was quite true that the originators of the move- , ment were not natives of Stockton Heath, nor did

MRS. DODDS,

MR. A. J. KING, M.P.,

(applause). Mr. Parker said he was proud they had bad Mr. Mounfield with them, for he was making Warrington and district famous by his researches and the, very interesting papers he was writing in the Warrington Press- He (the speaker) was interested to know that a few years after the first total abstinence society was founded at Stockton Heath, temperance workers from Warrington and Stockton Heath invaded Renketh with banners flying, drums beating, and comets blowing, and formed a society there which had done a very good work in the village—not, perhaps, under the original name, but the cause had been continued up to the present time and he thought they were as temperate a people at Penketh as they were even at Stockton Heath (Laughter.) During tt.e eighty years which had giapsed since the foundation of the first society, the ternper- ance standpoint had been gradually made stronget. For a long time people thought they could not live without intoxicating liquor, or at any ,fate that they would not live so long, but it Dad been proved by the statistics of life assurance companies that they actually lived a great deal longer. They could, in fact, live healthy, hearty and happy lives without intaxicants. (Applause.) Mr. Dodds said he rejoiced to be present although personally he believed that money for religious pur- poses should be raised, if possible, without resonrce to bazaars. However, he never pleaded that as an excuse for refusing to help a bazaar and he wished them every EUCCeSS in their effort, It was said that temperance was the handmaid of religion, and there was no vloubt that in connection with their church it had been so. The success which had attended the temperance effort must be a source of encouragement and ,joy to the ,friends there, especially in view of the fact that the first Temperance Society in England was formed in that room. (Applause.) Mr. A. Mounfield, in expressing thanks to Mrs. Dodds and Mr. King, observed that that building had remained unchanged since the memorable days just seventy-nine years ago when the movement began. Then it was the village chapel and it was filled with pews, whilst just below the spot,now marked by the tablet stood the pulpit. Joseph Livesey, of Preston, deliveted his famous malt lecture there, and Henry Anderton, Hugh Bourne, William Clowes, and other famous men had preached the cause from the old pulpit. That chapel and Mount-street Meeting-house, Manchester, were the only two buildings now remain- ing of those associated with the beginning of total abstinence. The old Cockpit of Preston and the old Friars Green Chapel at Warrington had gone, to they were gathered that day in a little historic building which stood as a permanent memorial of the beginning of a world-wide movement. Mr. Mounfield remarked that as women were not talking on that early occasion—(laughter)—their names were not recorled on the tablet, but uevertheless.they gratefully acknowledged the part which women played in the beginnings of the movement. He had received a letter of apology for non-attendance from the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, of Burtonwood, and it was interesting to note that Mr. Thomas, a predecessor of his, travelled to Stockton Heath to hear the new doctrine, and his name was inscribed in the roll book. Mr. Mitchell would gladly have come, but he would not have bazaars at any price. (Laughter.) However, in his letter he found consolation in saying they were not beyond the pale of blessing, and he hoped blessing would rest on their efforts. Alderman Elennett was prevented from attend- ing, owing to bereavement. In conclusion, Mr. Moral- field asked Mrs. Dodds to accept as a souvenir a medallion of John Wesley which had been prepared by a great grand niece of the famous evangelist. The vote was heartily accorded and Mrs. Dodds and Mr. King having responded, the business of the bazaar began. The roll books of the first society containing the abstinence pledge and nearly 2,000 autographs were dis- played in a glass case, Mr. Mounfield also displayed a copy of the first manifesto, the early cartoons (which appear on another page) and some relics of the Eaton and Clarke families. Thomas Eaton, it was explained, encouraged temperance by keeping a stone jug filled with a free and perpetual supply of buttermilk for the boatmen of the Bridgwater Canal, and the old jug and cup were exhibited. The following are the names of the stallholders : Ladies' Sewing Class : Mesdames Isherwood,Tlawley, Cantrell, Pearse and Cain. Crockery Stall : Mrs. A. Mounfield: Toffee Stall : Mrs. Bra.yshaw. Young Ladies' Stall : Members of the young ladies' Bible class. Yourig Men's:Stall : Members of the young men's Bible class. Refreshment Stall and Boom: Mesdames Houghton, E. Woods, Roughsedge, Misses Mounfield, E. Strange Massey and Barton. Flower and Plant Stall: Mr. Lain and daughters. The general arrangements committee consisted of Messrs. Hawley, Pearse, Roughsedge, Cantrell,, Rams- bottom, Jackson, Syers, Grounds and Mounfletd. Mr. A. Mounfield was the chairman of the bazaar committee, and Mr. W. H. Grounds the financial secretary.

THURSDAY'S PROC'EEDINGS.

The proceedings were opened on Thursday by Mr. T. Robinson, of Stretford, who gave an encouraging and entertaining address. Mr. Alderman Bennett in an interesting speech expressed his gratification that a memorial had beep raised to the memory of the temperance pioneers. He paid tribute to the labours of Mr. Mounfield in the sphere of historical research and and rejoiced that his efforts had won" for Warrington im ierishable fame as the true home of the Total .A bsticence movement. He was only sorry that Stockton Heath and not Warrington, that Cheshire and not Lancashire had the honour., ' The net proceeds of the Bazar were £130: This sum includes a genercui contribution of £l5 from Mr. James Dodds,and £5 each from Mr. A. J. King M.P. and Mr. John Cann.

they belong to the Independent Methodist Church. 1 It was gratifying to him, as a member of the Society of Friends, to find that two of the men who came to Stockton Heath with that mission were Quakers. It was also somewhat fitting that one of them was an Irishman, because Ireland bad always been honourably associated with the great temperance question. They all knew the work of Father Matthews in that direction. But although these good men were not Independent Methodists and one came from Ireland and the other from Manchester, it was the Independent Methodist Society which first gave Them a welcome, found them a home, and enabled them to set on foot that great move- ment which in the last eighty years had almost trans- formed the face of society in this country. He did not know whether he read with greater interest the hccount of the foundation and growth of the total abstinence society, or that of the growth of their church. They had many points in common, for both began with very simple and humble beginnings and were inaugurated and carried on by men who felt they had a message to deliver, and who were not discouraged and disheartened by difficalties. They persevered with their work in face of.all kinds of discouragement and obstacles. And after all, was not that

THE HISTORY or ALL GREAT REEORMS and had it not a lesson for them to-day? He thought the lesson it taught was that they should never be i afraid of being n a minority, of standing up for an unpopular cause, or of being called dreamers or idealists. What would the world have been without its dreamers and idealists? A second lesson was that of individual, responsibility. They were sometimes too apt, when they were faced with some great sooial evil or problem that wanted tackling, to say "That is very sad but, after all, lam only an individual. It is a matter for the Government." All great reforms, however, had been in the first place, the work of some enthusiastic individual. People hardly realized what a tremendous influence one man might have by appealing to what was best in the instincts of his fellows. It was wonderful bow people would respond to such an appeal when made by one who gave them the impression of honesty to his convictions and the truth of what he was saying. The most impressive temperance sermon he ever heard was delivered by a lady It was many years pgo when be was a boy at school and the lady visifbd Scarborough. He did not recollect much of what she said but he did recall the text and the burden of her remarks. She was dwelling on that very point of the responsibility of every individual to do what he could in his own sphere to remedy the evil, and the text she kept repeating was "What part or lot in this matter belongs to me?" Those words he had never forgotten, and when they were constantly being faced with great problems they would do well to put that auestion to themselves. One could give many instances from history. ===== 0002601_19090501_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
ABRAHAM LINCOLN.

When Lincoln set his hand to that great work of main- taining the Union on a basis of freedom by abolishing slavery, he was a comparatively poor and unknown man and his first task was to stand for his part of the country with the idea of representing it in the Senate. His opponent was a powerful speaker, amm n of great influence and eloquence—Senator Stephen Douglas. Early in the campaign one of Lincoln's friends, who was rather afraid that he bad taken up a bigger task than he could carry through, asked him if he knew who the man was that be was going to light. " Yes" was Lincoln's reply " Senator Douglas is a man with thousands of blind /followers and it is my work to make those followers see." That was the kind of spirit which ' overcame great difficulties and brought about great results. That was the spirit which actrated their forefathers in the founda- tion of a total abstinence society and in the building up of their church at Stockton Heath, and the best wish that he could wish them to-day was that they in their generation Might carry on their work in the same spirit. He was sure that if they did sn, they would be gratified by equally great results. here was plenty of work to do with regard to the temperance question. Much as the country had improved, we were spending far too much on drink. The

===== 0002601_19090501_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
DRINK BILL

was still. £160,000,000. People told us we could not afford to do away with that because the revenue of the country derived some 414,000,000 a year from the drink trade. All he could say was that, like all other forms of indirect taxation, it was a very expensive way of paying their taxes, because for every £1 they paid to the State they paid £4 out of their own pocket for purposes which were of very little profit to anybody concerned. We were told by the highest authorities that 75 per cent. of our crime and pauperism was due to drink. The, N.S.P.C.C. declared that 90 per cent. of the cases which came before them—and they were far too many—were directly traceable to drink. Therefore there Iva& still work to be done. They must ' not forget that whilst drink was the cause of so much misery in the world, misery was very largely the cause of drinking in this country, and in flg4ing the question they must fight it not only directly by attackipg the question itself but indirectly by doing what they could in all directions to lift up the conditions and circumstances of the lives of the masses of the people, to see that they were better housed, better educated, and better fed, and then he believed they would do something to reduce the temptations to drink. (Applause.) Mrs. Dodds then unveiled the memorial, and in the course of a short address said she deemed it the greatest possible compliment and honour that could have been paid to her to be asked to perform that cere- mony. Probably lb 'fin their lives and in all communities there Were certain spots which they termed holy ground, and she was quite sure the friends at Stockton Heath would aTways feel that place was holy ground because the tablet would preach to them, as it wete, so many sermons and bring before them so many memories. As to tazaars, she

believed the people who said the most against them had never found anything just to take their place. It was a much easier thing for those who had it to give money, but it was not everybody who c,uld afford to do that and, after all, it was not the people who gave the money who made the bazaars successful but those who worked behind the scenes week by week and year by year, to keep up the.church by their own efforts. She trusted the workers at Stockton Beath would be rewarded and that the sale would realize their utmost expectations. (Applause.) The Rev. C. Harvey Cook said the tablet would stand there as a silent witness in the days to come, bringing before tbem the words of the good old Book "They being dead yet speak." In that remarkable book ECclesiastious, there was the most elaborate effort to express what one age owed to another in the 44th chapter which said: "Let us now praise famous men and our fathers who begat us." This As very good, but we lived in another time and it did not content him that we praised our fathers. He had learnt anotherlesson—to praise his mother. It was,in his opinion, a sad omission that there was not a woman's name on the tablet. He thought the women must have been in it—(laughter)—in fact be found the names of many were entered in the roll book. In unveiling that memorial, continued Mr. Cook, they were not flouting the "Seven men of Preston" who, it was claimed, established the first total abstinence society in England in 1832. They were not detracting from tb 3 glory of those men, but they wished to do 'honour to those who were first in the field, and were first to establsh a dis- tinctly total abstinence society. Tbe:e • had been temperance societies which abstained from spirituous liquor and allowed malt to be taken, but the Stockton Heath Society was unique because it made no exception. In Mr. Mounfleld's book there was a reference to the fact that no church in the neighbotwhood would allow those pioneers of eighty years ago to hold their teetotal meetings. He (NTr. Cook) was jealous for the name of his own church, because they_had had to buy their liberty and position at a great price, and he would like to be quite sure whether his predecessors at Cairo-street were asked, because be could not imagine that they could have been approached. Of all the people who would give opportunity to any- body to express what their souls felt he was sure the predecessors of the people amongst whom he ministered would be ready to open the door to them. Mrs. PARKER said Mr. Harvey Cook was a splendid champion of the women's cause, but she was not sure whether the women were not getting hold of him to such an extent that he was becoming rather rough on the men. (Laughter.) Probably the women were not so aggressive in the early days of the temperance move- ment.lt bad taken this century to bring women on to the platforms to plead their cause. She was sure Mr. Mounfield and the friends at Stockton Heath would be the last ones to do anything whichewould seem to be a slight on the women who had done a great work.

The Examiner, Saturday May 1, 1909.

A STRIKING BUDGET.

LEVY ON MOTOR CARS, LAND, PIRITS, TOBACCO, LARGE INCOMES,

AND CLUBS.

GOOD NEWS FOR FAMILY MEN.

Mr. Lloyd George, in introducing his Budget on Thursday afternoon spoke four hours aid a half, with an interval of half an hour. His main proposals for raising the revenue deficit of nearly sixteen millions were as follow : MOTOR•CARS.—The proposed tax is based on horse- I power, and ranges from 2gs. for cars under 61-h.p. to 20gs. for cars over 60-h.p. A tax of 3d. per gallon on petrol ia also proposed. INCOME-TAX.—NO change in the tax on earned incomes up to £3,000 is proposed. Incomes now liable to is. duty are to be charged is. 2d. On incomes under £5OO an abatement of £lO per child for all children under sixteen years is to be allowed. In the case of incomes over £5,000 a super charge of 6d. in the pound will be made on the amount by which they exceed £3,000. ESTATE DUTIES.—It is proposed to shorten the steps and steepen the gradients between the limits. Thus an estate of between £5,000 and £lO,OOO will pay 4 per cent., one between £lO,OOO and £20,000 5 per cent. and one between £BOO,OOO and £1,000,000 14 per cent. At present £1,000,000 pays 10 per cent. The settled estate duty is to be raised from Ito 2 per cent. Legacy and succession duties will vary according to the degree of relationship of the Leneficiary from 5 to 10 per cent. When there is no relationship the duty will be 10 per cent. LICENCES.—The minimum duty on licensed houses in rural and urban districts with populations of less than 2,000 is to be £5; with populations betweeu 2,000 and 5,000, £10; between 5.000 and 10,000, £l5; 10,000 and 50,000, £2O; 50,000 and l 00,000,30; over 100,000, £35. At preseat the licence is £4 10s. IN hen the annual value is under £lO, and it rises to £6O according to the annual value. Clubs are to pay 3d. in the pound on liquors sold. Brewers and distillers will pay according to their production, and other manufacturers and dealers will pay increased duties. LAND TAxEs.—Twenty per cent. of the unearned increment of land is to be taken by the State, starting ' with the valuation as at present. , A tax of Id. in the pound is to be imposed on the the capital value of undeveloped land and mining , royalties. SPIRIT DuTv.—The duty is to be raised from,lls. per proof gallon to 14s. Dd. The duties on imported spirits will also be increased by 3s. 9d. per gallon or Gd. per bottle. TOBACCO DUTY.—The duty on unmanufactured tobacco, which at present ranges from 3s.to 3s. 4id. per pound, is to be increased by Bd.per pound or a halfpenny per ounce. STAMP DUTTES.—There are also increases in the stamp duty for the transfer or sale of property, in the duty on bearer certificates, and on the transfer duty on stocks and shares.

COUNCILLOR J. FULTON-SMITH.

Sir,—The Budget proposals present so runty novel and intricate divergences from ordinary business that much more explanatory matter is needed before it is possible to form a fair estimate of the consequences of the new order of taxation. At first sight the Budget proposal portends very heavy burdens upon the middle-plass, as also serious disturbance to the trade of the country. The high license duties appear to be based upon the American system, but it should not be forgotten that in the United States the liquor duties are much lower than in this country.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
J. FULTON-SMITIL

MR. JAMES DODDS.

Sir,—So far as I have had time to study it, the Budget marks a new departure. It is a democratic one. Wbases its provisions mainly on the needs of social reform. It puts the burden of taxation upon the right shoulders, upon the shoulders which can most easily carry it. The additional burdens are largely assessedin proportion to the ability of those who have to bear them, and the principle of graduation which has come to stay, is further developed. Those whose taxable capacity is higher, are to bear higher rates of taxation. This is as it ought to be, and as it is going tote ! The rich will not feel the burden of the increased calls upon diem, certainly they need not! But if they do, they should receive comfort from the thought that they are at last, though under compulsion, following the Scripture injunc- tion "Bear ye one anOther's burdens." No doubt the labouring classes have hitherto had to pay a much larger proportion of their income in rates and, taxes than: have the middle and upper classes. This ought not to be ; indegd up to the level of bare sub- sistence there should be no taxation: Two of what may be called luxuries of the poor are to pay intreased taxation—spirits and tobacco—but non-Users may escape the imposts, perhaps, in the case of spirits at any rate, to their advantage ! The taxes on great income's, the license duties the motor-car tax, and above all the tax on the future unearned increment on urban land render the Budget a great and historic‘one, and I wish it God-speed! On one point in- Mr. Lloyd, George's speech I .do not agree with him. He expressed surprise that last year's income tax yielded so much,as it was, lie said, a year of bad trade. That is so, but a large part of the tax received last year was in respect of the aver- age trading profits of the previous three years, or in the case of collieries of the previous five years several of which were really years of good trade. JAMES DODDS. Warrington, April 30th.

===== 0002601_19090501_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. A. H. CROSFIELD, M.P.

Sir,—l received your message asking me to send you to-night some comment on the Budget. The debate upon it has already commenced this evening and is proceeding as I write these few lines towards eleven o'clock, though the big guns of different parties (with the exception of an important intervention by-Mr ,Redmond) are reserving, their .criticisms till they have had some chance to digest the immense amount of material—some of it undoubtedly* —.controversial material—contained in Mr. Lloyd George's speech. Let me just enumerate a few of the really great questions included in this bold, memorable and com- prehensive Budget. First of all, there is a great extension of the principle of graduated taxation in the, shape of a very large addition to the Income Tax to be paid by the fortunate possessors of large incomes. Then there is an equally radical revision of the death duties carried out on most progressive lines. A third question of the first magnitude, the Taxation of Land Values, is also dealt with, not so much with a view to any large and immediate increase in the revenue to be derived from • this source, as with to a view increas- ing revenue from it in the future. Faced as we are with the absolute necessity of raising an extra sixteen millions (speaking in round numbers) of taxation, it has .been felt that the State created monopoly of the Liquor Trade should at such a time bear its fair share in meeting this national necessity. As in the case uf other main sources of taxation above mentioned the contribution from the Liquor Trade will be a very substantial one, but I. may take this opportunity of drawing attention to the fact that Mr. Lloyd-George's rate of taxatton is on the nominal and not .on the real values of licensed premises ; that the interests of hotels and bona-fide clubs are safeguarded—the latter in an ingenious way —while the interests of 'owners of licensed premises will also be i ngeniously safeguarded froitn unfair and unchecked competition by less reputable clubs ; and the interests of the- working licensed victualler in tied houses are also carefully looked, after. If I said nothing more—and there is it vast deal more contained in the Budget—even the above brief summary as it stands would show how extensive and far reaching Mr. Lloyd-George's financial proposals are. That fact strikes one at once. Mr.. Lloyd- George has faced the music and produced an exceptional 'Budget to meet an exceptional situation, but What is even more striking than the com- prehensiveness of his Budget is its democratic character. The burden of National and Imperial defence on the one hand, and Social Reform—may I not also call it 'Social Regeneration—on the other hand, these are shared and rightly shared by all classes of the com- munity. But at the same time, throughout Mr. Lloyd George's speech, which lasted four and a half hours, . there was manifest the whole time the broad principle that this burden should in the main rest on the shoulders of those who can best afford to bear it. I have often frankly put it in Warrington that this Parlia- ment is the most genuinely democratic Government I that Britain has ever had, and the Budget introduced ' to-day is worthy of that character. Therefore whether I modified in this that or the-other particular, as it may easily be in the • dourse Of the long debates on it , thronkhout this - summer—it is in substance and in I principle deserving of the support of every man and every woman who has at heart- the welfare of the nation.—Yours etc.,

A. H. CROSFIELD.

MR. A. J. KING, M.P.

Sir,—You ask me to send you a few lines on the Budget statement. It is now 6 o'clock and the statement is only half

through. The House has adjourned for half an- hour and if lam to catch this post I shall not be able to report on the whole scheme.. I may perhaps make few observations on the proceedings so far.- I have never known the House so crowded on any former occasion. The Chancellor appeared to me to be nervous,:and at times could not be distinctly heard. He was, as usual, a long time in coming to the point. He commenced by a summary of the commitments of the Government, not only for this year but for the next and the following years. He made it clear that this year's naval programme involved necessarily a largely increased expenditure next year whether the number of Dreadnoughts be four or eight. He also made it clear that the Government do not intend to allow this expenditure on naval and military preparations to delay their programme for social reform. Money must be found for both, and taxes must be imposed which, while they provide the necessary millions for this year, will beV capable of expansion and provide millions for future years. The tax on motor ears, to be devoted to the improvement of main roads, will be welcomed by local authorities.

Money is also to be provided far the encouragement of scientific research in Agriculture, and if necessary for promoting the development of the country. I think the Chancellor is right in dismissing the proposal to reduce the age limit for Old Age Pensions, and looking rather to some .scheme of universal com- pulsory insurance._ _ _ .

As 17v as-expected the Income Tax and Death Duties provide a large part of the increased revenue—two- pence extra on incomes over £3,000 and a super tax on incomes over £5,000. Death duties are stiffened up and the maximum of 15 per cent. comes into force on all estates of £1,000,000 or over. _

So far we have had nothing of Licensing or Land Tax and I must leave your readers to find this out from to-morrow's paper. Some of the provisions are complicated and we cannot predict how they will work out without furthe consideration.—Yours &c.,

House of Commons, April 29th. 9

ALFRED J. KIN(,

MR. C. E. PARKER

Sir,—The budget is altogether too big to admit of anything of the character of a review, and it is best that I should concentrate my remarks on one feature, a feature, which I venture to suggest is the most import- ant in the whole scheme. I refer to the modest com- mencement for the taxing of those increasing land values which are in no way due to the efforts of the owner. • Air illustration given by the Chancellor of the Exchequer puts the thing in a nutshell. "There was the case of the Festiniog slate quarrymen, who had to build on rocks.tilat would not feed a goat and upon swamps from which the landrord could not, and did not, receive more than 2s. an acre, or at the outside is. Gd. These were let to the quarrymen for building

purposes at rents that amoupted. to 50 an acre. Bui that was not all. The,leases were given f9r 60 years. All the improvements were effected by the quarrymen themselves, or by the local authorities to whom they paid their, rates. As the men Paid their mortgages, to the building societies the pro- perty, instead of increasing in value for them, became of less and less Value year by year as it passed into the possession of the landlord." Or talip another case given. An estate in the Woolwia district which was bringing in an income ,of £750 per annum or_a capital value of £15,000 was now through the selection of the district for Government works

bringing in an annual income in rents about equal to the original capital value, and according to the present law of leases the property built by the nation upon this ground would revert back to the landowner's family, so that out of an original value of £15,000 a sum of £2,000,000 would be netted by the landowners Cases on similar lines could be given a good deal nearer home, Now what is the Budget proposal in regard to this increase? It is proposed by the new tazc to appropriate one-fifth or twenty per cent of the future increase value. It is to apply to

future , appreciation of value only and ft- - will not touch any increment already accrued. No doubt the cry of Confiscation will be raised, but all reason- able men will Admit the claim which the Chancellor makes is a very moderate one. It is simply this, that a proportion of the increased value should be given to those who create it, that wealth earned by idleness— although this way of putting it suggests an absurdity— should Contribute a substantial proportion to. those expenses necessary to safeguard that wealth, from dangers arising from foes from within and without

the country, The second proposal in regard to land is on similar lines and it is proposed to impose a tax of a halfpenny in the pound on the capital value of all land not used to the best advantage, and this also includes minerals. Thus if an owner is holding back from use land or mines required by the people, holding it back in pros- pect of an increase which he believes the community will have ultimately to pay,- he must either bring the land and mines into use or pay the halfpenny in the pound on the capital value. The only fault I have to find in the tax is that it is not high enough. Still it . . .

will have the effect of bringing in-to tiocral and com- mercial use a good deal of land and minerals which at present are being witheld in hopes of higher prices The third proposal affecting land is the imposition ! of a 10 per cent. reversion duty upon any benefit accruing to a lessor from the determination of a lease. That is, supposing there is a GO years lease by which under present law the land and any property built upon it by the lessees goes, at end of lease, to the ground landlord, he will be • made to pay 10 per cent. of the value to the/ national expenses. I do not think there would have been any hardship if the lessor had been obliged to pay back to he lessee a substantial -proportion of the value of the property which had been built at the expense of the lesser. For this we must wait. It is very difficult righting at one , Stroke a system which, even although entirely wrong in principle, has been acknowledge& by law and the value recognised as the basis of sales, distribution, . .

mortgages, loans, inheritance, &c. • Taken as a whole the Budget is a splendid one. There is not an item in the whole list which will hamper trade or discourage entfrprise. It has left untaxed the necessaries of life but .it has tapped

luxuries. The burdens fall mainly -upon idle wealth which grows Fithout effort and which levies its tribute on the energies of others. The weight is adjusted with a carefully calculated incidence mainly on those things which are a super- fluity and luxury. The Budget shows that instead of the resources of Free Trade being drained the greatest resource of Free Trade is in _freer - land, and the

bringing into common use of that which is witheld from its best use by the whim or avarice of the owners. The Budget as a whole (though it may require improvement on minor points) deserves the whole-hearted and strenuous support of the Party, and this will not be withheld. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, April 30th, 1909.

MR. C. E. PARKER.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Claudian."

This pld, which pays a .welcome return visit to the Royal Court Theatre this week, is one of the late Mr. Wilson Barrett's most popular creations and its novel and even weird story enchains interest from beginning' to end. The picturesque sdays of old Byzantium possess a peculiar fascination, and the play has all the pomp and ,dash of military display as wellas the more saddening episodes of the slave market and the terrors of the earthpiake—the latter a veri- table triumph in stage craft, so l realistic as to send al thrill of alarm through those who undergo it for the first .time. Mr. William Maclaren as Claudian, is superb. His commanding presence, powerful and flexible voice,and rare dramatic skill eminently fitlim for the exacting part and each evening he has been accorded an ovation. Miss Lily , C. Bandmann (daughter of Mrs. Bandmann-Palmer) is also a great favourite as Almida, her characterisation being marked with depth 'of' feeling, and sufficient restraint. Mr. Douglas Earle plays the rough and brawny smith Agazil with splendid freedom and power and Mr. Ernest A. Foster presents a clever and convincing study of Thariogalus,the scheming Tetrarch, who meets with well deserved retribution. The cern- pany is a capable one all round—in fact there is not a weak link in the chain—and the stage effects are remarkably good.

We are Tailors to Business Men. Sportsmen know us well and say no firm can produce a pair of Riding Breeches such as we can; the Clergy for miles round wear our Broad-cloth and bear testimony to the beauty of our tailoring ; you will see our Liveries and Uniforms dotted about the town and we dress the "man of fashion " to his . heart's content. But Sportsmen, Clergymen and Dudes are not a big constituency and will certainly not keep us going. No! The Business Man is our best customer and you will find that our Ranges are selected with this in view. Glance at these Dressy, Tasteful Cashmeres and these Genuine Scotch Tweeds in New Colorings. They are the cloths a man should wear to look his best and as though he was keeping abreast of the times, and besides being nice in appearance they are both IREDWEARING and INEXPENSIVE. THE REVOLUTION IN TURKEY. . - THE SULTAN ATTENDING SERVICE IN THE HAMIDIE MOSQUE, CONSTANTINOPLE. Mt While the Young Turks were knocking at the gates of Constantinople the Sultan W2f Ilding service in the Hamidie Mosque, and this unique picture shows Court officials and Zuce spies surrounding the entrance, while outside are drawn up the soldiers of the First Corps, who remained faithful to Abdul Hamid. This photograph was taken on ay, and it was during their attendance outside this Mosque, that the Young Turks agered Constantinople without having to fire a shot.
